Title: Accurate waveforms for generic planar-orbit binary black holes: The multipolar effective-one-body model SEOBNRv6EHM
Authors: Aldo Gamboa, Alessandra Buonanno, Lorenzo Pompili, Raffi Enficiaud, Michael Boyle, Lawrence E. Kidder, Oliver Long, Peter James Nee, Harald P. Pfeiffer, Antoni Ramos-Buades, Mark A. Scheel
Published: 2026-05-27
Categories: gr-qc, astro-ph.HE

Accurate waveforms for generic planar-orbit binary black holes:
The multipolar effective-one-body modelSEOBNRv6EHM
Aldo Gamboa,1, * Alessandra Buonanno,1, 2 Lorenzo Pompili,3, 1 RaffiEnficiaud,1 Michael Boyle,4 Lawrence E.
Kidder,4 Oliver Long,1 Peter James Nee,1 Harald P. Pfeiffer,1 Antoni Ramos-Buades,5, 1 and Mark A. Scheel 6
1Max Planck Institute for Gravitational Physics (Albert Einstein Institute), Am M¨ uhlenberg 1, Potsdam 14476, Germany
2Department of Physics, University of Maryland, College Park, MD 20742, USA
3Nottingham Centre of Gravity&School of Mathematical Sciences, University
of Nottingham, University Park, Nottingham NG7 2RD, United Kingdom
4Cornell Center for Astrophysics and Planetary Science, Cornell University, Ithaca, New York 14853, USA
5Departament de F´ ısica, Universitat de les Illes Balears, IAC3 – IEEC, Crta. Valldemossa km 7.5, E-07122 Palma, Spain
6Theoretical Astrophysics 350-17, California Institute of Technology, Pasadena, California 91125, USA
(Dated: May 28, 2026)
Accurate and computationally efficient waveform models are required to infer the parameters of compact
binaries from their gravitational wave (GW) emission. Among these parameters, orbital eccentricity serves as
a smoking gun for dynamical formation channels and must be accounted for to avoid systematic errors in GW
analyses. Here, we presentSEOBNRv6EHM, a time-domain, multipolar waveform model for binaries on generic
planar orbits, calibrated to quasi-circular (QC) numerical-relativity (NR) simulations from theSXScollabora-
tion. In addition to the dominant (2,2) mode, the model provides the (2,1), (3,3), (3,2), (4,4), and (4,3) mul-
tipoles for the full inspiral-merger-ringdown process of coalescing binaries, as well as for dynamical captures
and scattering encounters. The model is built within the effective-one-body (EOB) framework, and it employs
novel resummations of the radiation-reaction force and waveform modes. We validate its accuracy through
comparisons against 592 QC, 319 eccentric, one dynamical-capture, and two scatteringSXSNR waveforms,
and through scattering-angle comparisons against 61SXSNR simulations. For QC and small-eccentricity bi-
naries, its accuracy is comparable to previous-generationSEOBNRv5models. For highly eccentric systems,
however,SEOBNRv6EHMattains unprecedented accuracy, with waveform mismatches remaining below or close
to 2% across the total mass range 20−200 M ⊙ for eccentricities up to∼0.9 at 14 periastron passages before
merger. Additionally,SEOBNRv6EHMachieves waveform-generation walltimes that are 2−6 times faster than
other state-of-the-art EOB eccentric models, enabling efficient and accurate applications in GW astronomy.
I. INTRODUCTION
Over 200 gravitational-wave (GW) signals from compact
binary coalescences have been observed by the LIGO-Virgo-
KAGRA (LVK) collaboration [1–10]. These GW events are
typically associated with binaries composed of black holes
(BHs) or neutron stars (NSs) moving on inspiraling quasi-
circular (QC) orbits. Such orbits are astrophysically expected,
as GW emission circularizes the binary [11–14] over the long
lifetime predicted for systems formed in isolation [15–21].
However, astrophysical formation channels also predict a
small but non-negligible population of merging binaries with
detectableorbital eccentricity. Specifically, the dynamical
formation channels are expected to produce eccentric bina-
ries that merge within the sensitive frequency band of current
LVK detectors [22–31] and future GW observatories [32–35].
These dynamical formation mechanisms are viable in dense
stellar environments where different types of gravitational in-
teractions become feasible, including von Zeipel-Kozai-Lidov
oscillations in hierarchical triple systems [36–44] andn-body
processes [45–54]. In this way, the presence of eccentricity in
GWs serves as a clean discriminator (asmoking gun) between
the isolated and dynamical formation channels [55–60].
Neglecting orbital eccentricity has widespread conse-
quences across different GW analyses. A substantial frac-
* aldo.gamboa@aei.mpg.de
tion of potential eccentric GW signals may be missed by
standard matched-filtering searches relying on QC waveform
templates [61–64], motivating the inclusion of eccentricity
in GW search strategies [65–76]. Similarly, analyzing data
containing an eccentric signal with QC templates can lead
to biases in the inferred binary parameters (e.g., masses and
spins) [77–86], as well as in the evidence for gravitational
lensing [87, 88], and in tests of general relativity [89–99].
Several GW events have been identified as poten-
tial eccentric candidates; however, their interpretation re-
mains debated, as different analyses often yield conflict-
ing conclusions. Some interesting candidates include: 1)
GW190701 203306 [100, 101], GW200129 065458 [100,
101], and GW231223 032836 [102, 103], which have support
for the eccentric binary black-hole (BBH) hypothesis but is
dependent on the specific glitch mitigation method [100, 103];
2) GW230712 090405 [102], whose support for the eccen-
tric aligned-spin BBH hypothesis reduces significantly when
compared to the QC precessing-spin hypothesis, thus point-
ing toward the known degeneracy between eccentricity and
spin precession [83, 84, 104–106]; 3) GW200208 222617,
associated with an eccentric BBH signal by several stud-
ies [100, 101, 107–109], but with high false alarm rate;
4) GW190521 030229, for which some studies find evi-
dence supporting eccentric [110, 111] or dynamical-capture
[112, 113] BBH hypotheses, while others find no conclusive
evidence [100, 114, 115]; and 5) GW200105 162426, a signal
from a neutron-star–black-hole (NSBH) binary with evidence
arXiv:2605.28715v1  [gr-qc]  27 May 2026

2
for eccentricity [116–119], for which there is a contrasting set
of results reported by different follow-up analyses [120–123].
The astrophysical implications of these potential eccentric de-
tections have been discussed, e.g., in Refs. [103, 124–127].
Disagreements concerning the presence of eccentricity in
GW events highlight the need to developaccuratewave-
form models (and better eccentric parameter-estimation tech-
niques). Numerical relativity (NR) provides the most accurate
eccentric GW templates by directly solving Einstein’s field
equations [78, 128–138]. However, producing enough NR
simulations to satisfy the demand required by GW analyses is
a highly expensive computational task, which is further com-
plicated by the two additional parameters needed to character-
ize an eccentric binary [139–144]. Only recently, enough NR
simulations have been produced to allow for the construction
of NR surrogate waveform models for eccentric nonspinning
BBHs [145–147]. Nevertheless, it remains crucial to comple-
ment NR simulations with semi-analytical waveform models.
Different approaches have been taken to develop eccen-
tric waveform models for comparable-mass binaries. Us-
ing post-Newtonian (PN) results for eccentric binaries [148–
169], Refs. [170–182] built inspiral-only eccentric models,
including spin-precession effects [183–187]. Based on such
results and information from the merger-ringdown of QC
NR simulations, Refs. [78, 131, 188–193] constructed hybrid
inspiral-merger-ringdown (IMR) eccentric models. Alterna-
tive methods use empirical properties of NR waveforms to
construct eccentric templates from existing QC models [194–
201]. Computationally efficient eccentric waveform approxi-
mants have been constructed within theIMRPhenomfamily of
models [202, 203]. Work on small-mass-ratio eccentric wave-
form modeling has been done, e.g., in Refs. [204–208].
Another approach to build eccentric waveforms is based
on the effective-one-body (EOB) formalism [209–214]. EOB
models combine analytical results of the two-body problem
[from the PN, post-Minkowskian, and gravitational self-force
(GSF) approximations] with calibrations to NR simulations
to produce highly accurate waveforms. Two families of EOB
models exist in the literature,SEOBNR(e.g., Refs. [215–236])
andTEOBResumS(e.g., Refs. [237–243]), and both have been
extended to model eccentric binaries (independent efforts have
been made in Refs. [244, 245]). Particularly, theTEOBResumS
family has done studies to improve eccentric waveforms both
in the comparable-mass and test-mass limits [246–266], in-
cluding extensions to spin-precession effects for BBHs [267],
for NSs [268, 269], and in parametrized deviations of general
relativity [270]. The model encapsulating all these develop-
ments is collectively known asTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı.
For theSEOBNRfamily, past eccentric mod-
els includeSEOBNRE[271–274] (extended to spin-
precessing BBHs [275]),SEOBNRv4EHM[276, 277], and
SEOBNRv5EHM[278, 279] (extended to a machine-learning
surrogate model [280]). Additional test-mass-limit studies
have been done in Refs. [281–285].SEOBNRv5EHMmodels
aligned-spin BBHs onboundeccentric orbits, and is about
an order of magnitude more accurate thanSEOBNRv4EHM
andTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı. In the large eccentricity limit,
however, the performance of these models degrades, with
mismatches close to or above 20% for highly eccentric, long
NR waveforms. Extending the applicability ofSEOBNRv5EHM
to generic planar orbits and improving its performance across
parameter space motivated the development of a new model.
By leveraging novel results for the EOB radiation-reaction
(RR) force and waveform modes, we developSEOBNRv6EHM,
a new time-domain, multipolar waveform model that is ca-
pable of modeling the dynamics and GW emission of BBHs
on generic planar orbits (see Fig. 1). IMR, dynamical-
capture, and scattering signals are modeled with the multi-
poles (ℓ,|m|)∈ {(2,2),(3,3),(2,1),(4,4),(3,2),(4,3)}, with
respect to a−2 spin-weighted spherical harmonic decomposi-
tion.SEOBNRv6EHMis calibrated only using QC simulations
from theSXScatalog [286], and follows the same methods for
constructing IMR waveforms asSEOBNRv5EHM, including a
QC prescription for the merger-ringdown phase.
SEOBNRv6EHMhas improved accuracy, particularly for high
eccentricities. We quantify the accuracy ofSEOBNRv6EHMby
comparing its predictions against waveforms and scattering
angles extracted fromSXSNR simulations. For QC and low-
eccentricity binaries,SEOBNRv6EHMshows the same overall
accuracy asSEOBNRv5EHM, but removes high-mismatch out-
liers that are present forSEOBNRv5models. For bound eccen-
tric binaries,SEOBNRv6EHMis thefirst and onlymodel whose
mismatches stay below∼2% foralleccentricity values when
comparing against 319 eccentric NR waveforms. This repre-
sents an order of magnitude improvement in accuracy for the
highest eccentricities with respect toSEOBNRv5EHM. For all
eccentricities,SEOBNRv6EHMis overall an order of magnitude
more accurate thanTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı. For unbound binaries
and a dynamical capture, the waveforms and scattering angles
predicted bySEOBNRv6EHMare in better agreement with NR
results than those ofTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı.
SEOBNRv6EHMhas a better computational performance
and has no inherent parameter space restrictions. Thanks
to code optimizations and a simpler analytical structure,
SEOBNRv6EHMapproaches the speed ofSEOBNRv5HMin the
QC-orbit limit, and achieves faster waveform-evaluation wall-
times by factors of∼2−6 with respect toSEOBNRv5EHM
andTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı, depending on the binary configura-
tion. In Bayesian parameter estimation, this translates into an
overall speedup of approximately a factor of three relative to
SEOBNRv5EHM, as shown in the companion manuscript [287].
Moreover,SEOBNRv6EHMintroduces a new set of equations
of motion that substantially enlarges the accessible parameter
space with respect toSEOBNRv5EHM.
The structure of this work is as follows. In Sec. II, we pro-
vide an overview ofSEOBNRv6EHM. In Sec. III, we derive a
new resummation of the RR force. Prescriptions for initial
conditions are given in Sec. IV. The construction of multipo-
lar waveforms of BBHs on generic planar orbits is presented
in Sec. V. The calibration to QC NR waveforms is explained
in Sec. VI. The accuracy, robustness, and computational speed
ofSEOBNRv6EHMare assessed in Sec. VII. We conclude in
Sec. VIII and discuss future work. We leave secondary results
to appendices: Appendix A provides expressions for various
quantities derived in this work, and Appendix B presents the
fits employed for the calibration parameters ofSEOBNRv6EHM.

II. OVERVIEW OF THESEOBNRv6EHMMODEL
Waveform generation in EOB models proceeds in two main
steps. First, the binary dynamics is computed using the EOB
formalism. Then, the waveform is constructed from analytical
expressions evaluated on the dynamics and, for coalescing bi-
naries, it is smoothly stitched to a phenomenological merger-
ringdown ansatz. Here, we summarize how these steps are
done within theSEOBNRv6EHMmodel.
A. EOB dynamics
The EOB formalism [209–214] is an analytical approach
that maps the gravitational two-body dynamics onto theef-
fective dynamicsof a test mass or a test spin in a deformed
Schwarzschild or Kerr background, with the deformation be-
ing parametrized by the symmetric mass ratioνand the spins
of the binarya 1 anda 2. The effective HamiltonianH effgov-
erning the dynamics of the effective particle is related to the
two-body HamiltonianH EOB via the energy map
HEOB =M
s
1+2ν
 Heff
µ −1
!
.(10)
We use the aligned-spin effective Hamiltonian derived in
Ref. [221], which was employed in theSEOBNRv5HM[220]
andSEOBNRv5EHM[278, 279] waveform models. This Hamil-
tonian is applicable to binaries on generic planar orbits, and
reduces exactly to the Hamiltonian of a test mass in a Kerr
background in the limitν→0. It resums the full 4PN in-
formation and most of the 5PN nonspinning contributions, as
detailed in Ref. [221]. Moreover, its accuracy is improved
by including two parameters,a 6 and ˆdSO, at high PN orders,
which are calibrated against QC NR simulations (see Sec. VI).
The back-reaction of GW emission on the binary dynamics
is modeled by incorporating a RR force into Hamilton’s equa-
tions of motion. This RR force is derived frombalance laws
that relate the GW fluxes of energy and angular momentum to

5
the corresponding losses of the system. To improve its accu-
racy, the RR force in EOB models isresummedusing various
physically and numerically motivated techniques.
InSEOBNRv6EHM, we use a new expression of the RR force,
whose azimuthalF ϕ and radialF r components are given by

The RR force (11) employs the EOB dynamical variables
(r, ϕ,p r∗ ,p ϕ), along with their time derivatives ˙r, ˙ϕ≡Ω, and

B. EOB waveforms
The plus and cross polarizations of GWs,h + andh ×, are
modeled through thewaveform modes h ℓm, which are the ex-
pansion coefficients of a decomposition in terms of−2 spin-
weighted spherical harmonics, −2Yℓ,m. Specifically,

To compute the waveforms, the factorized modes (21) are
evaluated on the binary dynamics obtained from the numeri-
cal integration of the equations of motion (15). For coalescing
binaries, these modes are supplemented with the nonquasi-
circular correctionsN ℓm, and are smoothly stitched to a QC
merger-ringdown, as indicated in Eq. (20). This procedure
yields highly accurate waveform modes for the entire inspiral-
merger-ringdown evolution of bound binaries, or for the scat-
tering process of unbound binaries.
III. RADIA TION-REACTION FORCE FOR BINARIES ON
GENERIC PLANAR ORBITS
The RR force plays a fundamental role in producing accu-
rate waveform templates, as it determines the binary’s phase
to which GW detectors are highly sensitive. In this section,
we motivate and derive a new resummation of the EOB RR
force applicable for binaries moving on generic planar orbits.
A. The PN-expanded RR force
The RR force can be derived from first-principle calcula-
tions or through a set offlux-balancerelations. The former
approach relies on direct integration of the retarded field gen-
erated by the binary [292–297], or on matching procedures
between the near-zone and wave-zone fields [298–305]. In

7
contrast, the flux-balance method [306–308] requires a con-
sistency between the (gauge-dependent) local energy and an-
gular momentum losses by the system, ˙Esys and ˙Jsys, and the
corresponding (gauge-invariant) GW fluxes of energy and an-
gular momentum at infinity,Φ E andΦ J. These quantities can
be related by introducing a pair ofSchott terms,E Schott and
JSchott, which represent additional gauge-dependent contribu-
tions arising due to the interaction of the system with the ra-
diation field, as in the theory of electromagnetism [309].
To determine the RR force, we start by writing the flux-
balance equations for generic planar orbits as [310]
˙Esys + ˙ESchott + ΦE =0,(23a)
˙Jsys + ˙JSchott + ΦJ =0.(23b)
Then, from the equations of motion (15) we get the relations
˙Esys =˙rFr + ΩF ϕ and ˙Jsys =F ϕ, which lead to
Fϕ =− ˙JSchott −Φ J,(24a)
Fr = −ΦE − ˙ESchott + Ω(Φ J + ˙JSchott)
˙r .(24b)
Afterward, one substitutes expressions for the fluxes (e.g.,
the 3PN EOB fluxes derived in Ref. [278]), and pro-
poses ans ¨atze for the Schott terms whose time derivatives
are obtained by substituting back the equations of mo-
tion (15). The Schott terms are parametrized by arbitrary
gauge constants{α i}and{β i}, such thatJ Schott(r,p r,p ϕ,{α i})
andE Schott(r,p r,p ϕ,{β i}). In this way, an expansion in small
velocities leads to a set of linear equations for{α i}and{β i},
whose solution can be expressed in terms of a reduced set of
independent gauge constants. This ultimately yields generic,
gauge-dependent PN expressions for the RR force compo-
nents in terms of EOB coordinates.
At leading order (2.5PN order), there are two independent
gauge constants, commonly denoted byαandβ, which allow
the RR force components to be written as [278]:

By using the gauge condition (28), the same ans ¨atze for
the Schott terms given in Eqs. (64) and (65) of Ref. [278], and
the energy and angular momentum fluxes derived therein,3 we
obtain complete 3PN expressions of the RR force for binaries
on generic planar orbits, as a function of the leading order
gauge constantsαandβ. For reference, the 1PN expressions
are given in Eq. (A15) of Appendix A, while the complete
3PN expressions, as well as the Schott terms, are provided in
the Supplemental Material. Such expressions generalize the
ones given in Eqs. (66) of Ref. [278], which can be obtained
by takingα=−16/3 andβ=−13/2.
B. A new resummation of the RR force
PN-expanded expressions perform poorly at high veloci-
ties, making them insufficient for modeling the plunge or the
periastron passages of binaries with moderate to high eccen-
tricities. For this reason, EOB models use resummations to
improve the robustness and accuracy of PN expressions. Here,
we propose a new resummation of the RR force that enables
accurate modeling of binaries on generic planar orbits.
2 In Ref. [278], the calculation was extended up to the relative 3PN order.
At 2.5PN order one needs to take into account the back reaction of the
leading order RR effects, which are associated to the constantsαandβ.
3 These fluxes are composed of instantaneous and tail contributions. The
instantaneous terms are valid for generic planar orbits, while the tail con-
tributions are derived in an expansion inp r. The original tail contribu-
tions to the fluxes are given in an orbit-averaged eccentricity expansion.
By employing ap r-expanded ansatz in terms of EOB variables, the orbit-
averaged tail contributions can be transformed into non-averaged expres-
sions. This transformation makes it possible to derive expressions for the
RR force that depend only on EOB variables.

on the orbital frequencyΩ. As a result, it becomes difficult to
accommodate the additional eccentric degrees of freedom and
the RR force inherent gauge dependence.

The eccentricity correctionsF ecc
ϕ andF ecc
r are obtained
by requiring that the PN expansion of Eqs. (42a) and (42b)
matches the 3PN RR force derived in Sec. III A. The under-
lying QC factorization in Eqs. (42) ensures that these correc-
tions satisfyF ecc
ϕ →1 andF ecc
r →1 in the QC-orbit limit.
For later convenience, we denote byF ecc, PN
ϕ andF ecc, PN
r the
explicit PN-expanded expressions, to distinguish them from
their resummed counterparts [see Eqs. (45)].
By construction, these expressions are consistent with the
leading order RR force for generic planar orbits (26), since
the correctionsF ecc
ϕ andF ecc
r start at relative 1PN order. In the
QC orbit limit [Ωc →Ω+O(ϵ 11),˙r2 → O(ϵ 10),˙pr → O(ϵ 10)],
this resummation satisfies the gauge in Eqs. (28), closely fol-
lowing the standard QC expressions (29). Furthermore, using
the QC modesh F, qc
ℓm without eccentricity corrections and as
functions of the instantaneous frequencyΩ, makes the evalu-
ation of the RR force highly efficient.
To improve the behavior of the eccentricity corrections
F ecc
ϕ andF ecc
r , we find it important to parametrize them in
terms of (r,˙r,˙pr∗,cons) and to perform an additional resum-
mation. Without such control, previous work found sig-
nificant numerical difficulties with the eccentric RR force,
which led to the total (inSEOBNRv4EHM[277]) or partial
(inTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı[252, 255], where the radial RR
force component contains a QC reduction of the 2PN re-

10
sults from Ref. [310]) removal of eccentricity corrections.
SEOBNRv5EHM[279] keeps complete eccentricity corrections,
but at the expense of introducing extra Keplerian parameters,
which limit the model’s applicability to bound orbits and in-
troduce potential issues in the equations of motion.
The parametrization (r,˙r,˙pr∗,cons) makes the RR force (42)
fully applicable to generic planar orbits and it enables an accu-
rate recovery of the underlying QC factorization, since ˙rand
˙pr∗,cons are very close to zero for QC inspirals. We find that
usingΩorp ϕ leads to worse performance, as these variables
have inherent noncircular contributions, and thus rely on PN
corrections to achieve a good QC-orbit limit. In contrast, ˙r
and ˙pr∗,cons are independent variables that naturally separate
the eccentricity contributions from the PN orders. We employ
˙rinstead ofp r∗ since the former remains smaller in magni-
tude throughout the entire evolution. The use of ˙p r∗,cons is
convenient, as it is directly obtained during the integration of
the equations of motion (15) and does not depend explicitly
on the dissipative dynamics, thereby avoiding a circular (im-
plicit) dependence on the RR force. The 3PN-accurate trans-
formation rules required to write all the relevant expressions
in terms of (r,˙r,˙pr∗,cons) are given in Appendix A 1.
The additional resummation applied toF ecc
ϕ andF ecc
r is a
novel proposal that guarantees robust numerical behavior dur-
ing the plunge and highly relativistic periastron passages. As
the PN order increases, the eccentric RR force becomes more
intricate, involving multiple terms with different powers of
three dynamical variables [e.g., see Eq. (A15)]. This becomes
problematic in high-velocity configurations, often leading to
issues in the integration of the equations of motion.
Specifically, the resummation takes the form:
F ecc
ϕ = 2
1+exp  F arg exp
ϕ
 ,(45a)
F ecc

1+exp  F arg exp
r
 ,(45b)
whereF arg exp

leading 2.5PN order), and are obtained from the relations
given in Appendix A 3, which ensure that the PN expansion
of Eqs. (45) is consistent with the expressionsF ecc, PN

Eqs. (A21) of Appendix A, while the full 3PN expressions are

This sigmoid resummation satisfies:
•F ecc

ϕ →0 and
F arg exp

•F ecc

during the plunge, ifF arg exp
ϕ → ∞orF arg exp
r → ∞,
•F ecc
ϕ →2 orF ecc
r →2 in strong periastron passages or
during the plunge, ifF arg exp
ϕ → −∞orF arg exp
r → −∞,
thereby always controlling the growth of the corrections.
3. Selecting a convenient RR gauge
Choosing a gauge is important for the accuracy and numer-
ical robustness of the RR force. In general, the gauge con-
stants can be mapped to specific coordinate choices, and can
take arbitrary values (within a certain class of coordinate sys-
tems), as explained in Refs. [306–308]. While all the gauges
are physically equivalent in the PN regime (weak-field, slow-
velocities), the situation is more complex during strong peri-
astron passages or the plunge, where highly relativistic veloci-
ties are reached. For example, Ref. [318] showed that different
RR gauges lead to significantly distinct features in the evolu-
tion, particularly when the eccentricity is high. Indeed, the
gauge constants determine the algebraic structure of the RR
force at each PN order [e.g., see Eqs. (A15)]. Thus, the gauge
has a non-negligible impact on the evolution of the binary.
Our gauge is determined by Eqs. (28) together with the
ans¨atze for the Schott terms from Ref. [278]. This leaves two
free gauge constants,αandβ. To fix them, we focus on the
denominators shown in Eqs. (A21), given by
dϕ =1− 2α+1
4
r˙r2
M + α+2
4
r2 ˙pr∗,cons
µM ,(46a)
dr = −3α−10
6 − α−β+2
2
r˙r2
M + α−3β−1
4
r2 ˙pr∗,cons
µM ,
(46b)
which, by construction, are associated with the leading order
terms of the RR force (26).
In Fig. 2, we show the evolution ofd ϕ (left panels) and
dr (right panels) across variousαandβvalues, for a fixed
highly eccentric binary dynamics. Particularly, we focus on
the gauge choice fromSEOBNRv5EHM[278] (α=−16/3,
β=−13/2), and a gauge that removes the factorr 2 ˙pr∗,cons
fromd ϕ andd r, such that
α=−2, β=−1.(47)
We see that some gauge constants (including the ones from
SEOBNRv5EHM) lead to vanishing denominators, making them
unsuitable for robust evolutions. In contrast, the values (47)
produce a regular behavior of the denominators. This can be
inferred from Eqs. (46), since for these values ofαandβ,d ϕ
andd r remain strictly positive and negative, respectively, for
all values of (r,˙r,˙pr∗,cons), thereby guaranteeing nonproblem-
atic denominators. Furthermore, in calculating the eccentric-
ity corrections to the RR force [e.g., Eq. (A21)], several terms
acquire a dependence on the factorsα+2 andα−3β−1, which
vanish in the gauge (47). This choice, therefore, leads to more
compact expressions that are faster to evaluate.
For these reasons, we use the gauge (47) inSEOBNRv6EHM.
We emphasize that this choice is based on properties of our
proposed RR force resummation (42). Other resummations
may benefit from similar arguments to select an appropriate
RR gauge. As discussed later in Sec. VII B 2, the gauge has
important effects on the accuracy of the model, and thus is
worth studying with more detail in future work.

4. TheSEOBNRv6EHMRR force
The RR force inSEOBNRv6EHMis obtained from Eqs. (42)
with the gauge condition (47), and 1PN contributions to
F arg exp
ϕ andF arg exp
r , as given in Eq. (A21). Additionally, some
of the factorized modesh F, qc
ℓm are augmented with calibration
parameters, as discussed in Sec. VI C. This results in the ex-
pression (11) presented before.
We restrict the eccentricity corrections to 1PN order, as
higher-order contributions degrade agreement with NR wave-
forms. A similar issue was observed during the early devel-
opment of theSEOBNRv4EHMmodel [277]. We conjecture that
this effect originates from the current paradigm for resumming
the eccentric RR force, namely, enforcing the standard QC
factorization∝Ω P m2dLhF, qc
ℓm
2
and supplementing it with
eccentricity corrections. Under such construction, the correc-
tions inherit a dependence on different powers ofΩ, leading
to increasingly intricate expressions at higher PN orders [e.g.,
the multiple terms proportional top n/3
ϕ,N in Eq. (A21)]. As a re-
sult, we noted a reduction in waveform accuracy, particularly
at low eccentricities. In contrast, we observed that the pre-
dictions for the scattering angle (presented in Sec. VII B 3 for
theSEOBNRv6EHMRR force) improve as the PN order of the
correctionsF ecc
ϕ andF ecc
r is increased. However, we consider
a more accurate waveform model for bound binaries to have
higher priority. Thus, we restrict the eccentricity corrections
to 1PN order, and leave for future work a careful study of the
effects of higher PN orders on model accuracy. Here, we em-
phasize that the QC part of the RR force (42) includes high-
PN-order information that partially captures eccentric effects
via its dependence on the instantaneous frequencyΩ. Hence,
it is not straightforward to quantify the impact of using 1PN
corrections on the phasing of eccentric inspirals.
This reflects the complexity of modeling eccentric bina-
ries and underscores the need for better resummations. Re-
laxing the requirement to recover standard QC factorizations
may prove advantageous in future work. This would allow the
RR force to naturally adapt to eccentric variables, eliminating
the need for ad hoc elements, such as theΩ c function (13).
The sigmoid resummation (45) is promising for incorporating
higher PN orders without compromising the robustness of the
expressions. Test-mass-limit studies could help assess differ-
ent RR force prescriptions, as in Refs. [246–250, 281].
IV . INITIAL CONDITIONS FOR BINARIES ON GENERIC
PLANAR ORBITS
An aligned-spin binary has four degrees of freedom, as en-
coded in the equations of motion (15). Thus, four initial val-
ues (r 0, ϕ0,p r∗0,p ϕ0) are required to uniquely determine the
binary’s evolution. Here, we discuss how these initial condi-
tions are computed from the input parameters given in Table I.
Specifically, we detail the procedures for determining initial
conditions 1) forbound orbits, given values of the dimen-
sionless orbit-averaged orbital frequency⟨MΩ⟩, eccentricity

12
e, and relativistic anomalyζ(or mean anomalyl), 6 and 2) for
generic planar orbits, given values of the energyE/M, total
angular momentumJ/M 2, and separationr.
A. Bound orbits
We determine initial conditions for bound orbits from start-
ing values of the orbit-averaged orbital frequency⟨MΩ⟩, ec-
centricitye, and relativistic anomalyζ, following a post-
adiabatic approach. Under this approximation the con-
servative (adiabatic) values of the dynamical variables,
(r0,cons ,p ϕ0,cons ,p r∗0,cons), are computed first, followed by dis-
sipative (post-adiabatic) corrections, (r0,diss ,p ϕ0,diss ,p r∗0,diss),
due to the effect of the RR force starting at 2.5PN order. This
results in the splitting of the initial values as
ϕ0 =0,(48a)
r0 =r 0,cons +r 0,diss ,(48b)
pϕ0 =p ϕ0,cons +p ϕ0,diss ,(48c)
pr∗0 =p r∗0,cons +p r∗0,diss ,(48d)
where we setϕ 0 to zero by convention.
InSEOBNRv6EHM, the conservative part of the initial values
is determined with PN formulas and a root solving procedure.
Specifically, the radial momentum is calculated with a 3PN-
accurate relation given in Eq. (B11) of Ref. [278], namely
pr∗0,cons(⟨MΩ⟩,e, ζ)= ⟨MΩ⟩ 1/3esinζ√
1−e 2
+O(1/c 2).(49)
Next, the values ofr 0,cons andp ϕ0,cons are determined by nu-
merically finding the roots of the equations
Ω(⟨MΩ⟩,e, ζ)= ∂HEOB[r,p r∗0,cons,p ϕ]
∂p ϕ
,(50a)
˙pr∗(⟨MΩ⟩,e, ζ)=−ξ(r) ∂HEOB[r,p r∗0,cons,p ϕ]
∂r ,(50b)
whereΩ(⟨MΩ⟩,e, ζ) and ˙p r∗(⟨MΩ⟩,e, ζ) are calculated with
conservative 3PN-accurate formulas, given in Appendix B of
Ref. [278]. The initial guesses of the root solver are calculated
with Eqs. (34b) and (34c) of Ref. [278].
The dissipative contributionsr0,diss,p ϕ0,diss , andp r∗0,diss, are
directly calculated with the 2.5PN terms given in Eqs. (40b-d)
of Ref. [278]. These terms have a dependence on the leading-
order RR gauge constantsαandβ, which we set to−2 and−1,
respectively, as discussed in Sec. III B 3.
This prescription for the initial conditions closely follows
the one employed in theSEOBNRv5EHMmodel, but changes
the way in whichp r∗0 is calculated. InSEOBNRv5EHM, the
6 Other models use areferencefrequency besides thestartingfrequency
(e.g., see Refs. [202, 203]). InSEOBNRv6EHM, the reference and starting
frequencies are the same, but we provide the option to integrate backward
the full equations of motion (15). In the companion manuscript [287], we
show the usefulness of this in parameter-estimation analyses.
procedure to determinep r∗0 involved another root-finding pro-
cedure, and it was designed to accurately recover the method
employed in the QC modelSEOBNRv5HM. This was important
to avoid spoiling the calibration to QC NR simulations inher-
ited fromSEOBNRv5HM. However, inSEOBNRv6EHM, we do not
have such constraint, since the model has been recalibrated.
Thus, we decided to use the (simpler) prescription for the ra-
dial momentump r∗0 described above, since it is less prone to
errors, compared to the method used inSEOBNRv5EHM, which
can be problematic when applied to highly eccentric binaries.
To ensure the generation of physically meaningful wave-
forms, we impose constraints on the allowed initial condi-
tions. Specifically, we prevent waveform generation if any
of the following conditions are met:
1) The initial angular momentum differs more than 1.5%
from its PN estimate given by Eq. (34b) of Ref. [278].
2) The initial separation differs more than 1% from its PN
estimate given by Eq. (34c) of Ref. [278].
3) The initial separation is less than 4M.
These conditions were determined empirically, and they ap-
pear for very challenging configurations, e.g., when the binary
is very close to merger, or when the eccentricity is very large at
high frequencies (for example, see Fig. 19 in Sec. VII D). The
first two conditions are also applied inSEOBNRv5EHM. Addi-
tionally, such model performs a backward integration of a set
of secular evolution equations{˙x,˙e,˙ζ}whenever the starting
separation of the binary is less than 10M. This allows the de-
termination of initial conditions at earlier (less challenging)
stages, and it was necessary to avoid problems with the equa-
tions of motion [279]. However, as discussed in Sec. VII D,
SEOBNRv6EHMdoes not perform such secular backward inte-
gration, and instead it imposes a cutoffin waveform genera-
tion whenever the starting separation is less than 4M, a regime
in which the approximations used to construct the initial con-
ditions are expected to be much less accurate.
Beyond the constraints on initial conditions,SEOBNRv6EHM
imposes additional requirements to ensure high-quality wave-
forms. In particular, waveform generation is aborted if the dy-
namics duration is less than 200Mand the final azimuthal an-
gle is less thanπ(meaning that the binary has completed less
than half an orbit), if the dynamics duration exceeds a thresh-
old determined by a background QC evolution (see Sec. V B),
or if the separation during the evolution significantly exceeds
(by a factor of 10) a Newtonian estimate. The first condi-
tion prevents unphysical features arising from an inaccurate
merger-ringdown attachment, while the latter two discard con-
figurations that are inconsistent with the intended physical
system represented by the input values ofe,⟨MΩ⟩, andζ.
Overall, these empirical conditions build up the robustness
ofSEOBNRv6EHM, which we discuss in Sec. VII D. We ex-
pect these conditions to change as better initial conditions and
more accurate evolution equations are available.

13
Mean anomaly l as an input parameter
The default radial phase parameter inSEOBNRv6EHMis the
relativistic anomalyζ, but we also provide the option to use
themean anomaly las an input parameter. The mean anomaly
is an angle that measures (linearly in time) the fraction of the
orbital period that has elapsed since the last periastron pas-
sage [78, 134, 145, 148, 319, 320]. Given an input value of
the mean anomaly, we employ Newtonian transformations to
obtain the corresponding relativistic anomaly, which is used
internally to compute EOB initial conditions as described
above. We note that this is the approach followed by the
TEOBResumS-Dal´ ımodel [259].
To determine the relativistic anomalyζfrom the mean
anomalyl, first we compute theeccentric anomaly uby nu-
merically solving Kepler’s equation,

To illustrate the differences between uniform distributions
in relativistic anomaly and mean anomaly, Fig. 3 shows the
corresponding distributions obtained by randomly drawing
107 samples of a given anomaly from the interval [0,2π] and
mapping them to the other anomaly at an eccentricity of 0.3.
Specifically, the top panel shows a uniform distribution in
mean anomaly and the corresponding relativistic anomaly dis-
tribution computed using Eqs. (51) and (52), while the bottom
panel shows a uniform distribution in relativistic anomaly and
the corresponding mean anomaly distribution computed using
Eqs. (51) and (54). We observe that a uniform distribution in
mean anomaly (cf. a uniform prior in mean anomaly, in the
context of parameter estimation), leads to a concentration of
the relativistic anomaly values aroundζ=π(apastron). Con-
versely, a uniform distribution in relativistic anomaly (cf. a
uniform prior in relativistic anomaly), leads to a concentra-
tion of mean anomaly values aroundl=0 (periastron). The
effects of relativistic and mean anomalies in the waveforms
are illustrated in Fig. 20 of Sec. VII D, where we see that the
mean anomaly parametrization leads to avisuallymore evenly
distributed waveform amplitude (see also Ref. [142]).

V . MULTIPOLAR W A VEFORMS FOR BINARIES ON
GENERIC PLANAR ORBITS
We model the GWs emitted by binaries on generic planar
orbits in terms of theirwaveform modes h ℓm. These are the
expansion coefficients of theh+ andh × polarizations in a−2
spin-weighted spherical harmonic basis decomposition (19).
We first numerically integrate the equations of motion (15),
and then compute the modes from analytical formulas evalu-
ated on the dynamics. For coalescing binaries, we employ a
phenomenological ansatz for the merger-ringdown phase.
More specifically, we decompose the modes according to
hℓm =


where the functionsh F

resum analytical PN results,N ℓm arenonquasicircular(NQC)
correctionswhich incorporate information from NR wave-
forms to improve the late-inspiral waveform of binary coales-
cences, andh merger-RD
ℓm is the merger-ringdown portion of the
waveform starting at the matching timet match related to the
peak of the (2,2) mode. We present all these elements with
more detail in the following subsections.
The multipoles supported by theSEOBNRv6EHMmodel are:
(ℓ,|m|)={(2,2),(3,3),(2,1),(4,4),(3,2),(4,3)},(59)
since for these we have information for the merger-ringdown
from QC NR simulations from Ref. [220]. These are the same
multipoles modeled in theSEOBNRv5EHMmodel [279].
The waveforms are aligned (t=0) at thelastpeak of
the frame-invariant amplitudeA inv, given a threshold value of
10% with respect to thelargestpeak ofA inv, where
Ainv =

|hℓm|2 .(60)
This convention allows the possibility that the last promi-
nent peak ofA inv (associated with the start of the merger-
ringdown) is not the largest one, which can occur for some
high-eccentricity binaries, as noted in Refs. [140, 262, 324].
The threshold of 10% is chosen to avoid selecting amplitude
peaks due to mode-mixing during the ringdown phase.
In the following, we describe the construction of multipolar
waveforms for binaries on generic planar orbits. We introduce
a new mode factorization, detail improvements to the inspiral-
plunge stage via NR-informed NQC corrections, and outline
the procedure for attaching the merger-ringdown phase.
A. Factorized EOB modes
Expressions for the eccentric modes have been derived
within the PN framework [162–165, 193]. However, their ac-
curacy and numerical robustness deteriorate at high velocities,
e.g., during the plunge or in strong periastron passages. To
improve this, EOB models resum the modes in terms of phys-
ically and numerically motivated factors [288, 311–313].
InSEOBNRv6EHM, we factorize the waveform modes as

well behaved, eliminating the need for ad hoc treatments, such
as the sigmoid functions employed in theSEOBNRv4EHM[277]
andTEOBResumS-Dal´ ımodels [252].
The factorsh ecc
ℓm are employed in a PN-expanded form up to
1PN order (relative to the leading-order of the (ℓ,m) mode).
During the development ofSEOBNRv6EHM, we tested higher
PN corrections. However, we found that their effect negatively
overlaps with that of the NQC correctionsN ℓm, since the am-
plitudes ofh ecc
ℓm grow considerably during the plunge. Such
issue is not observed inSEOBNRv5EHM[279], since it employs
a different prescription forh ecc
ℓm ; this enables the incorpora-
tion of higher PN orders, but restricts the model applicability
to bound orbits and introduces nonphysical features in highly
eccentric waveforms (see Fig. 13). This motivates adopting
the new approach at 1PN order. We emphasize, however, that

16
the factorh F, qc
ℓm (Ω) contains high-PN-order QC information,
which induces additional eccentric effects through the instan-
taneous frequencyΩ. This may provide a sufficiently accurate
approximation to the full expressions for generic planar or-
bits. Future work could explore alternative resummations or
parametrizations that enable the incorporation of higher PN
corrections without interfering with the NQC corrections.
For scattering orbits, Eq. (61) determines the full multipo-
lar emission modeled inSEOBNRv6EHM. No phenomenologi-
cal corrections are included, due both to the lack of a suitable
framework for their implementation and to the limited avail-
ability of NR scattering waveforms. Future work could ad-
dress this as more NR data becomes available.
For coalescing binaries, we improve the late-time wave-
form and attach a merger-ringdown, as described next.
B. NQC corrections and background QC dynamics
The inspiral-plunge part of the modes for coalescing bina-
ries is computed as

whereN ℓm are phenomenological NQC corrections which en-
sure that the amplitude and frequency of the modes agree with
input values coming from fits of QC NR simulations.
SEOBNRv6EHMadopts the same approach asSEOBNRv5EHM
for the computation of the NQC corrections using aback-
ground QC dynamics. Specifically, it employs the same func-
tional form for the NQC corrections as in Ref. [220], but eval-
uates them on the dynamics of a QC binary with the same val-
ues of masses, spins, and starting orbit-averaged frequency as
the eccentric system. To reduce computational cost, this QC
dynamics is obtained from a post-adiabatic evolution of the
equations of motion (15) [219, 330–333]. For this evolution,
we employ Eqs. (19) and (20) from Ref. [220], modifying only
the last term in their Eq. (20) to be used with a generic radial
RR force component,F r, to match our prescription (11b).

The NQC corrections (73) become relevant only during the
late inspiral and plunge. In particular, they satisfyN ℓm →1 in
the early inspiral since|p r∗,qc /µ| ≪r qcΩqc during a QC evolu-
tion. This would not be the case if Eq. (73) were evaluated on
an eccentric dynamics, since the quantityp r∗/(µrΩ) oscillates
throughout the inspiral. This problem was addressed in the
SEOBNRv4EHMmodel by using orbit-averaged dynamical vari-
ables (¯r,¯pr∗, ¯Ω) [277], and in theTEOBResumS-Dal´ ımodel
by using a sigmoid function that tapers the effects of the NQC
corrections in the early inspiral [252].
We find the use of a background QC dynamics more con-
venient since it is independent of the eccentric dynamics (e.g.,
the time to merger, or the eccentricity in the late inspiral), and
it helps in the determination of the merger-ringdown match-
ing time, as discussed in Sec. V C. Furthermore, the time to
merger of the QC dynamics,t qc
merger, can be used as a thresh-
old to prevent the generation of non-physical waveforms. At
fixed initial orbit-averaged frequency, eccentric systems are
expected to have a shorter time to merger,t ecc
merger, due to the
enhanced loss of energy and angular momentum through GW
emission. Therefore, an eccentric system witht ecc
merger >t qc
merger
would indicate inaccuracies in the initial conditions of the dy-
namics or in its subsequent evolution. Thus, inSEOBNRv6EHM,
we restrict waveform generation to the systems that satisfy
tecc
merger ≤1.05t qc
merger, where we added an empirically deter-
mined factor to account for different stopping conditions be-
tween the eccentric and QC dynamics.
As inSEOBNRv5EHM, the eccentric and QC dynamics are
aligned at a common value of the binary’s relative separation,
rref. The final dynamics in both systems is very similar thanks
to the binary circularization [11–14]. Therefore, we assume
that there is a timet ∗ such that, for all subsequent timest≥t ∗,
we haver≈r qc, whereris the separation of the eccentric
binary andr qc is the separation of the QC binary. Thus, we
align both dynamics at the timest ref, ecc andt ref, qc at which
r(tref, ecc)=r qc(tref, qc)=r ref,(74a)

for large eccentricities, as demonstrated by the comparisons
against eccentric NR waveforms shown in Sec. VII B. How-
ever, the assumptions may break down for extreme configura-
tions. Future work could focus on developing other methods
to determine the NQC corrections without resorting to orbit
averages, sigmoid functions, or additional binary evolutions.

The value ofr ISCO is calculated using the final mass and spin
of the remnant BH, which are determined with fits obtained
from QC NR simulations [335, 336]. We remark that the value
oft ISCO calculated from Eq. (77) is independent of features in
the late dynamics, and is uniquely defined since the separation
decreases monotonically in a QC binary.
This method for determining the matching timet match is the
same as inSEOBNRv5EHM. For eccentric binaries, one cannot
employ directly Eq. (76) to determinet match since 1)∆t 22
ISCO is
calibrated to QC NR waveforms, and 2) eccentricity oscilla-
tions could result in multiple roots of the equationr(t)=r ISCO,
which would make the model unstable under perturbations
of the input parameters. Therefore, given the absence of a
calibration to eccentric NR waveforms, the prescription in
Eqs. (75) for the matching time is a good strategy that lever-
ages the background QC dynamics (used in the computation
of the NQC corrections) to inherit the calibration done with
QC NR waveforms. Future investigations could focus on find-
ing alternative methods to compute the matching time, poten-
tially introducing a calibration to eccentric NR simulations, or
by having a reference point in the late dynamics of generic co-
alescing systems to which one could add a calibration param-
eter, as in theTEOBResumS-Dal´ ımodel (e.g., see Ref. [260]).
D. Merger-ringdown waveform modes
We employ a QC prescription for the merger-ringdown
modes of coalescing binaries. We use the same phenomeno-
logical ansatz for the merger-ringdown as inSEOBNRv5HM, in-
formed by QC NR simulations and QC test-mass limit wave-
forms [220]. The ansatz physically represents a Kerr BH that
vibrates down to a state of equilibrium, emitting damped os-
cillations known asquasinormal modes(QNMs). Specifically,
it takes the form [215, 216, 220, 337]

FollowingSEOBNRv5HM, another effect modeled in the
merger-ringdown ofSEOBNRv6EHMis themode mixingbe-
tween the (3,2) and (4,3) modes. Due to a mismatch between
thesphericalharmonic basis and thespheroidalharmonics
basis employed in the computation of QNMs in BH pertur-
bation theory [214, 339], the (3,2) and (4,3) modes get mixed
with other modes, and this leads to postmerger oscillations.
In contrast, the modes (ℓ,m)={(2,2),(3,3),(2,1),(4,4)}
of comparable-mass binaries show a predominant monotonic
amplitude and frequency evolution.
More investigation is needed to understand the biases intro-
duced by applying a QC prescription to the merger-ringdown
of eccentric binaries. Even at relatively high eccentricities,
deviations from a QC merger-ringdown remain at most at the
percent level [142]. However, one naturally expects this to
be a source of error for highly eccentric binaries [340], which
motivates future research directions. Additionally, it is known
that QNMs can be excited by scattering encounters or highly
eccentric periastron passages (e.g., see Refs. [341, 342]). We
leave the inclusion of these effects for future work.
VI. CALIBRA TION TO QUASI-CIRCULAR W A VEFORMS
Model accuracy can be improved through calibration to
waveforms generated by NR or BH perturbation theory. This
process extends the validity of models beyond the range of
their underlying approximations (e.g., low-velocity or small-
mass-ratio assumptions). For EOB models, it is possible
to calibrate both the binary dynamics and the corresponding
waveform. Identifying gauge-invariant quantities for the cal-
ibration process is straightforward in the QC-orbit limit, and
has been done, in particular, in previousSEOBNRmodels. In
the following subsections, we describe the calibrations to QC
NR waveforms performed inSEOBNRv6EHM.
A. Calibration summary
We calibrateSEOBNRv6EHMto 425 QCSXSNR waveforms
following a nested-sampling approach. Namely, we deter-
mine posterior distributions for specific calibration parame-
ters, which are then fitted with multidimensional functions.
We employtencalibration parameters: 8 seven in certainρ ℓm

The main differences with respect to theSEOBNRv5models
are: 1) we employ a different calibration parameter  ˆdSO
 for

ISCO, S,
instead of cubic polynomial fits, since this improves the over-
all performance of the model; and 3) we adopt a hierarchical
fitting strategy for spinning systems, first constructing fits for
equal-mass configurations and then extending them to unequal
masses, resulting in improved accuracy.
B. Calibration framework
We employ the nested-sampling methods presented in
Refs. [215, 220]. Here, we specify the metrics, settings, and
the data set employed for the calibration.

9 We note that the calibration is done using a specific PSD (in particular,

19
2. Nested-sampling analysis
FollowingSEOBNRv4[215] andSEOBNRv5[220], we adopt
a calibration strategy based on stochastic sampling to obtain
a posterior distribution for the calibration parameters for each
NR simulation. This approach provides an efficient and flex-
ible calibration procedure, well suited to the dimensionality
of the problem and the large number of NR simulations avail-
able. Stochastic sampling methods allow for an exploration of
high-dimensional parameter spaces, and have the advantage of
providing information regarding the correlations between cal-
ibration parameters. As inSEOBNRv5, we obtain the best com-
putational performance by employing nested sampling [347],
using the samplernessai[348] throughBilby[349].
We employ a likelihood function designed to minimize the
mismatch between EOB and NR waveforms while simulta-
neously improving the prediction of the merger time (the
mismatch alone is not particularly sensitive to this quantity).
Thus, we define the likelihood function as:
L(hNR |θ)∝exp
−1
2
 MmaxM(θ)
σM
!2
− 1
2
 δtmerger(θ)
σt
!2 ,
(84)
whereM maxM(θ) is the maximum mismatch between EOB
and NR waveforms over the range 10 M⊙ ≤M≤300 M ⊙, and
σM andσ t are tolerance parameters that control the effective
width of the likelihood. For this work, we set a mismatch goal
ofσ M =10 −4 for nonspinning configurations,σ M =5×10 −4
for spinning configurations, andσ t =5Mfor all cases. In
SEOBNRv5HM,σ M was set to 10−3 for all cases [220]; here, we
used smaller values ofσ M to facilitate the identification of the
correct modes in multimodal posteriors (see next paragraph).
Achieving a mismatch goal of 10 −4 forallbinary configura-
tions would require revisiting the calibration procedure and
modifying the analytic structure of the model, since not even
the maximum-likelihood points for all calibration data lie be-
low this threshold. Our objective here is to get a model with
accuracy comparable to that ofSEOBNRv5HM.
For each NR simulation in the calibration data set, we ob-
tain aposterior distribution, which is employed to construct
fits for the calibration parameters as functions of the binary
parameters,θ(Π). The mean and variance of these posteriors,
as well as the correlations among the calibration parameters,
are related to the requirements onσ M andσ t. In some cases,
these correlations lead to secondary modes in the posteriors
(see Fig. 3 of Ref. [220]); for such cases, we employ continu-
ity considerations to select only one mode to get a more reg-
ular fit across the parameter space. For all posteriors, we dis-
card samples that do not satisfy the calibration requirements
onσ M andσ t. If this would discard more than 50% of the
points, we instead keep half of the original samples of the se-
lected mode with the best likelihood values.

We adopt a different procedure from that used in
SEOBNRv5HM. In particular, we employ ahierarchical spin-
ning approach, where the equal-mass cases are fitted first, fol-
lowed by the unequal-mass cases (e.g., see Ref. [259]). This
approach is particularly useful forSEOBNRv6EHM, as it pro-
vides greater flexibility in fitting the data observed for systems
with close-to-equal masses (q≈1), as discussed below.
We perform a nested sampling analysis over the parameters

This suggests a further classification of the 78 equal-mass sim-

and different-spin configurations display a behavior that is not
possible to fit with simple, low-order polynomials, due to the
structure seen fora + ∈[0,0.5].
This behavior motivates the use of a more flexible fitting
strategy than that used inSEOBNRv5HM, leveraging the differ-
ent spinning configurations of equal-mass binaries. This hi-
erarchical spinning approach proceeds as follows: first, we
fit the equal-mass, equal-spin cases as a function ofa + only,
and the equal-mass, opposite-spin cases as a function ofa −
only. Next, we fit the equal-mass, different-spin cases using
the residuals from the previous fits. Finally, the unequal-mass
cases are fitted using the results of all previous fits.
Specifically, these hierarchical spinning fits take the form:

An advantage of this splitting is that it allows
SEOBNRv6EHMto satisfy the expectedspin-exchange
symmetryfor equal-mass binaries: the dynamics and wave-
form of equal-mass systems must remain invariant under
the interchangeχ 1 ↔χ 2. TheSEOBNRv5models fail to
reproduce this property, because the fits for the parameters
dSO and∆t 22
ISCO, S lack the required symmetry; instead, these
fits rely on the approximate behavior inherited from the
calibration data. In contrast,SEOBNRv6EHMrespects this
symmetry by enforcing it in the fitting procedure.

We performed extensive tests of the model and its fits to
ensure physical robustness across broad regions of parame-
ter space, including eccentricity. These tests are discussed in
Sec. VII D. To guard against overfitting, we also performed
comparisons with additional QC and eccentric NR simula-
tions not used in the calibration, as well as with QC NR surro-
gate models, as detailed in Sec. VII B. Moreover, the reliabil-
ity of the calibration pipeline for the multidimensional spin-
ning fits was validated in Sec. E of Ref. [220]. Overall, these
results indicate that the fits inSEOBNRv6EHMachieve the same

23
level of robustness as the establishedSEOBNRv5HMmodel.
Our results highlight the complex interplay between the
calibration process, fitting strategies, and model components
(Hamiltonian, RR force, and waveform modes). For exam-
ple, the calibration parameters∆ρ (1)
ℓm discussed in Sec. VI C
improve the model accuracy because they facilitate the over-
all calibration-fitting procedure, both inSEOBNRv5HMand
SEOBNRv6EHM. Future work may explore alternative calibra-
tion and fitting strategies, assess the role of individual model
components in the calibration process, and extend the calibra-
tion to NR or small-mass-ratio eccentric waveforms.
VII. MODEL V ALIDA TION
In this section, we assess the accuracy, speed, and ro-
bustness ofSEOBNRv6EHMacross different parameter space
regions. Accuracy is quantified through waveform and
scattering-angle comparisons with NR results, efficiency
through waveform-generation benchmarks, and robustness
through an analysis of the regions whereSEOBNRv6EHMyields
physically meaningful and accurate waveforms. In the com-
panion manuscript [287], we perform further tests within the
context of Bayesian parameter estimation, confirming the high
accuracy and computational efficiency ofSEOBNRv6EHM.
A. Waveform metrics
Thestrainmeasured in a GW detector can be expressed as
h(t)=F +(Θ,Φ,Ψ)h +(ι, φ,d L,Π,t c;t)
+F ×(Θ,Φ,Ψ)h ×(ι, φ,d L,Π,t c;t),
=A(Θ,Φ) (h+ cosκ+h + sinκ ) ,(100)
whereF + andF × are the detector’s antenna-patterns [343,
344],h + andh × are the GW polarizations defined in Eq. (19),
A(Θ,Φ) is given in Refs. [216, 217], andκ=κ(Θ,Φ,Ψ) is
an effective polarization angle. The strain depends both on
extrinsicandintrinsicparameters of the source. The extrinsic
parameters determine the location of the source with respect
to a GW detector: the angular position of the line of sight mea-
sured in the source frame (ι,φ), the sky location of the source
in the detector frame (Θ,Φ), the polarization angleΨ, the lu-
minosity distance of the sourced L, and the coalescence time
tc. The intrinsic parametersΠdetermine a unique physical
configuration: the binary massesm 1 andm 2 (or, equivalently,
mass ratioqand total massM), the dimensionless spin com-
ponents along thezdirectionχ 1 andχ 2, as well as an orbital
eccentricityeand a radial phaseζspecified at a certain stage
of the binary’s evolution (e.g., at an orbit-averaged frequency
⟨MΩ⟩), or a set of reference values for the energyE/M, angu-
lar momentumJ/M 2, and relative separationrof the binary.
To quantify the agreement between a templateh t (e.g.,
an EOB waveform) and a signalh s (e.g., a NR waveform),
we employ thefaithfulnessfunctions from Sec. IV A of
Ref. [279]. In general, these functions maximize the over-
lap (80) between the template and signal,
F(M,Σ s)=max
Σt
⟨hs|ht⟩,(101)
by optimizing the template parametersΣ t at a certain total
massMand for specific source parametersΣ s. For inner-
product calculations, we use the settings discussed below
Eq. (81). For comparisons against eccentric NR waveforms,
however, we interpretf start as the initial orbit-averaged fre-
quency of the NR (2,2) mode and we trim the longest wave-
form to ensure that both waveformsh NR andh t have the same
time to merger. This approach ensures that only physically
meaningful frequencies are considered, thereby avoiding con-
tamination from Fourier transform artifacts [220, 222, 279].
The template parameters optimized in Eq. (101) depend on
the type of binary (QC or eccentric) and on the mode con-
tent [e.g., (2,2)-mode only or including higher-order modes].
For QC waveforms, the coalescence timet c, azimuthal angle
φ, and effective polarization angleκof the template are opti-
mized [216, 217, 372–374]. For eccentric waveforms, addi-
tional optimizations are required due to the gauge dependence
of orbital eccentricity in general relativity.
The standard approach for comparing eccentric waveforms
is to optimize over the template’s eccentric parameters [187,
202, 203, 277, 279, 375, 376] (another approach, based on
time shifts, is presented in Ref. [137]). Here, we follow the
procedure described in Sec. IV A 3 of Ref. [279], in which
the faithfulness is numerically optimized over a grid of ec-
centricities and initial orbit-averaged frequencies with a fixed
radial phase parameter. Particularly, the radial phase is set
to apastron,ζ=π, as it avoids waveform-generation issues
at high eccentricities across all the considered models. This
brute-force optimization is performed only for the (2,2) mode
and for the lowest total mass under consideration.
When including higher-order modes, we use thesky-and-
polarization-averaged, signal-to-noise-ratio (SNR)-weighted
faithfulness[216, 217],
F SNR(M, ιs)=

where SNR= √⟨hs|hs⟩. These integrals are computed
as a discrete sum over a uniform grid of 8×8 values for
φs, κs ∈[0,2π] with a fixed value ofι s.

this way, lower mismatch values imply better agreement be-

Figure 5. Relative velocity (top panel) and separation (bottom panel)
for a set of NR equal-mass, nonspinning BBH configurations with
varying eccentricity: QC (SXS:BBH:2325, blue), moderately ec-
centric (SXS:BBH:2521, yellow), highly eccentric (SXS:BBH:2527,
red), dynamical capture (SXS:BBH:4000, violet), and scattering en-
counter (SXS:BBH:4292, orange). The dashed line in the top panel
marks 30% of the speed of light, underscoring the highly relativistic
nature of eccentric systems. The systems are aligned such thatt=0
corresponds to the peak of GW emission. Here,r i andv i (i∈ {1,2})

B. Comparison against NR simulations
In general, eccentric systems present a significant model-
ing challenge, as their periastron passages involve small sep-
arations and high relativistic velocities. This is illustrated in
Fig. 5, which shows the relative velocity (top panel) and sepa-
ration (bottom panel) for a subset of equal-mass, nonspinning
NR simulations with varying eccentricities. State-of-the-art
approximants accurately model the GW emission from QC
binaries (blue curves) up to velocities of order a few tenths
of the speed of light (for reference, we indicate the value of
30% by a dashed line), corresponding to a certain number of
orbits before merger. As the eccentricity increases, however,
the binary becomes progressively more relativistic at perias-
tron. This is evident in moderately (yellow curves) and highly
eccentric (red curves) simulations, whose periastron passages
reach velocities comparable to those reached by QC binaries
at the very end of their inspiral. Importantly, these highly rel-
ativistic configurations arise even during the early inspiral of
long-lived systems with moderate-to-high eccentricities, since
the major change in the dynamics of these binaries occurs at
apastron, while the velocity and separation at periastron re-
main relatively constant throughout the binaries’ evolution.
Therefore, accurate modeling of eccentric waveforms requires
capturing the strong-field, high-velocity binary dynamics to
prevent error accumulation at periastron passages.
Here, we quantify the accuracy of theSEOBNRv6EHM
dynamics and waveforms for different BBH systems. Specifi-
cally, we compute waveform mismatches against sets of QC
and eccentric NR simulations, we compare against NR wave-
forms from a dynamical capture and scattering encounters,
and we compute scattering angles for different configurations
for which NR data is available. All the NR waveforms and
scattering angles were computed with theSpECcode [366]
from theSXScollaboration [286, 350–365]. We also include
predictions from the state-of-the-art eccentric, aligned-spin
modelsSEOBNRv5EHM[279] andTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı[259].
For waveform comparisons, we include all modes supported
by each model. Namely, forSEOBNRv5EHMandSEOBNRv6EHM
we use (ℓ,|m|)∈ {(2,2),(3,3),(2,1),(4,4),(3,2),(4,3)},
and forTEOBResumS-Dal´ ıwe employ the modes
(ℓ,|m|)∈ {(2,2),(3,3),(2,1),(4,4)}. 11 For NR waveforms,
we include all modes up toℓ=4. Results forSEOBNRv5HM
[220] in QC waveform comparisons are identical to those of
SEOBNRv5EHM, and thus are not included.
1. QC binaries
We compute waveform mismatches against a set of 592
BBH QCSXSNR waveforms [286]. The parameter space dis-
tribution of these waveforms is shown in Fig. 4. A summary
of each NR waveform is provided in an ancillary file.12
The (2,2)-mode mismatches are computed with Eq. (33) of
Ref. [279] over a range of total massesM∈[10,300] M ⊙, and
the results are shown in the top panels of Fig. 6. We observe
an overall consistency between the accuracies of bothSEOBNR
models, with a slightly better median mismatch, a better con-
trol of mismatch outliers, and a slightly improved accuracy
for nonspinning waveforms withSEOBNRv6EHM. Addition-
ally, we observe an overall accuracy improvement of one or-
der of magnitude for bothSEOBNRmodels with respect to
TEOBResumS-Dal´ ı. The most challenging cases correspond
to systems with high mass ratios and/or high spins. However,
the mismatches ofSEOBNRv6EHMfor all these systems remain
below 3×10 −3, thanks to its analytical content, the higher-
order fits of the calibration parameters inSEOBNRv6EHM, and
the augmented calibration set with respect toSEOBNRv5EHM.13

The accuracy of GW polarizations (with higher-order
modes) is quantified with the sky-and-polarization-averaged,
SNR-weighted faithfulness (102) [see also Eq. (32) of
Ref. [279]], calculated for an inclinationι s =π/3, over the

curacy compared toSEOBNRv5EHM, and shows a significant
improvement with respect toTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı. In partic-
ular, the latter model underperforms in many cases, even re-
sulting in mismatches of∼40%. By contrast, the mismatches
ofSEOBNRv6EHMremain always below 1%. We note that
all models exhibit reduced accuracy for systems with high
mass ratios and/or high spins, as already pointed out, e.g., in
Ref. [220]). Furthermore, the models also show an increase in
mismatch with respect to the total mass, indicating that the
number of modeled higher-order modes and their accuracy
(particularly in the late inspiral) need to be improved [235].
ogous fits and the same calibration set as inSEOBNRv5EHM. The final
fits and the selected NR simulations for the calibration ofSEOBNRv6EHM
were chosen to get the best performance across different binary configu-
rations, as discussed in Sec. VI.
We also observe a spread in the SNR-weighted mismatches
of nonspinning waveforms (orange curves) forSEOBNRv6EHM
relative toSEOBNRv5EHM(compare bottom-left and bottom-
middle panels of Fig. 6). Thus, some multipolar nonspinning
cases inSEOBNRv6EHMare underperforming. In contrast, for
the (2,2) mode, there is an overall improvement for nonspin-
ning cases inSEOBNRv6EHM. This behavior is likely related
to the arbitrary choices made when defining the Newtonian
prefactors in Eqs. (69). Since these mismatches stay below
∼10 −3 and are still comparable to those ofSEOBNRv5EHM, we
defer the investigation of improved Newtonian prefactors for
higher-order modes to future work.

generation GW detectors [235].

the zero-eccentricity limit is at the same order as other state-
of-the-artdedicatedQC models. 14 We remark that developing
an eccentric model with an accurate QC limit is highly impor-
tant to avoid biases in parameter estimation [115, 379].

waveform mismatches against a catalog of 319 BBH eccen-
tricSXSNR waveforms. Of these, 184 (composed of 159
nonspinning and 25 aligned-spin configurations) are publicly

We calculate mismatches against NR waveforms following
the methods summarized in Sec. VII A. The three models,
SEOBNRv5EHM,SEOBNRv6EHM, andTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı, are
treated equally to ensure a fair comparison. The best-fitting
parameters for each model and each eccentric NR simulation
are provided in the ancillary file to this work (see footnote 17).
The results are presented in Fig. 11 for the three models
in separate columns. Each panel shows the mismatch as a
function of the total mass (M∈[20,200] M ⊙) corresponding
to the best-fitting waveforms for each NR simulation. The
color of the curves corresponds to the initial GW eccentricity
of the NR waveform, and we use dashed curves to indicate
spinning configurations. The top row shows the (2,2)-mode
mismatch [calculated with Eq. (34) of Ref. [279]], while the
bottom row displays the sky-and-polarization-averaged, SNR-
weighted mismatch (which includes higher-order modes) at a
source inclination ofι s =π/3 [calculated with Eqs. (36) and
(37) of Ref. [279]]. In each panel, we overlay a horizontal,
dot-dashed, blue line indicating the corresponding median of
all the maximum mismatches over the given total mass inter-
val. For the (2,2)-mode mismatches, the median is 0.029%
forSEOBNRv5EHM, 0.023% forSEOBNRv6EHM, and 0.37% for
TEOBResumS-Dal´ ı. For the SNR-weighted mismatches, the
median is 0.081% for bothSEOBNRv5EHMandSEOBNRv6EHM,
and 1.48% forTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı.

The results shown in Figs. 11 and 12 indicate that
SEOBNRv6EHMis, overall, the most accurate model across
the considered configurations. In the low eccentricity limit,
the mismatches ofSEOBNRv6EHMare comparable to those of
SEOBNRv5EHM. Since many of the employed NR waveforms
have low eccentricities, the minimum, medium, and maxi-
mum mismatches across the considered range of total masses
remain very similar between these models, for both the (2,2)-
mode and SNR-weighted mismatches (see Fig. 12). However,
as eccentricity increases,SEOBNRv5EHMprogressively loses
accuracy, with mismatches rising to∼20% at the highest ec-
centricities. In contrast,SEOBNRv6EHMkeeps the maximum
mismatches below∼2% foralleccentric NR waveforms in
theSXScatalog. The difference in accuracy becomes even
more pronounced when compared toTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı, for
whichSEOBNRv6EHMachieves mismatches that are an order
of magnitude smaller across all configurations.

vanishing multipoles{(2,2),(3,2),(4,4)}for the best-fitting
waveforms of the three models under consideration (the cor-
responding input parameters are given in the ancillary file;

31
see footnote 17). For a total mass of 20 M ⊙,SEOBNRv6EHM
yields a mismatch ofM 22 =2.1%, whileSEOBNRv5EHMand
TEOBResumS-Dal´ ıgive significantly larger values ofM22 =
19.5% andM 22 =30.6%, respectively.SEOBNRv6EHMis
therefore able to accurately capture the bursts of radiation at
each periastron passage, where the relative velocity reaches
up to 40% of the speed of light (see Fig. 5). In contrast, both
SEOBNRv5EHMandTEOBResumS-Dal´ ıpredict a less faithful
evolution, characterized by wrong intervals of time between
each periastron passage. Moreover, these two models exhibit
unphysical features in the higher-order modes:SEOBNRv5EHM
shows an incorrect amplitude behavior during the early in-
spiral, whileTEOBResumS-Dal´ ıdevelops zero-crossings in
the (4,4) mode near merger. In comparison, the higher-order
modes ofSEOBNRv6EHMdisplay a well-behaved evolution,
even qualitatively outperforming the NR modes, which are af-
fected by numerical noise due to their small amplitudes.
The accurate modeling of highly eccentric binaries is en-
abled thanks to the new analytical ingredients employed in
SEOBNRv6EHM. Particularly, the novel resummation of the RR
force, the choice of the RR gauge, and the use of a suit-
able parametrization, played a key role in achieving small
mismatches.SEOBNRv6EHMemploys 1PN eccentricity cor-
rections to the RR force and waveform modes, compared
to the 3PN expressions used inSEOBNRv5EHM. Despite this,
SEOBNRv6EHMyields a clear improvement for high eccentric-
ities. As discussed in Sec. III B 4, better resummations and
parametrizations are needed to include higher PN orders in a
way which benefits the model accuracy.
Another aspect to note in Fig. 11 is that some spinning
configurations are better modeled inSEOBNRv5EHM. This be-
havior originates from the choice of the RR gauge (discussed
in Sec. III B 3). During the development ofSEOBNRv6EHM,
we observed that using theSEOBNRv5EHMRR gauge (i.e.,
α=−16/3 andβ=−13/2) leads to the same improved mis-
match behavior for these spinning configurations. However,
the mismatch for the highly eccentric configurations increases
significantly (for example, the mismatch for the highly eccen-
tric simulationSXS:BBH:2527goes from∼2% to∼15%,
compared to the∼20% ofSEOBNRv5EHM). Furthermore, as
commented in Sec. III B 3, theSEOBNRv5EHMgauge could
lead to numerical problems with our resummation of the RR
force (42) (see Fig. 2). For these reasons, and considering
that the mismatches ofSEOBNRv6EHMfor the most demand-
ing spinning configurations are still within the∼2% level, we
decided to keep a different RR gauge (namely,α=−2 and
β=−1). We additionally note that this behavior is not re-
lated to the QC spinning calibration. For instance, one of the
spinning simulations with high mismatch inSEOBNRv6EHM
has a mass ratioq=2, dimensionless spinsχ 1 =0.8 and
χ2 =0.8, and initial GW eccentricitye GW =0.48. The QC
counterpartSXS:BBH:0618has the same intrinsic parameters,
but the maximum mismatch forSEOBNRv6EHMis∼0.073%,
whileSEOBNRv5EHMhas∼0.084% (i.e.,SEOBNRv6EHMmod-
els slightly better this QC configuration). Overall, these re-
sults point to the need for further investigation into the impact
of the RR gauge on the accuracy of EOB models.
Apart from the RR force, the modeling of higher-order

Figure 14. Mismatch between NR waveforms with only the (2,2)
mode and those including all modes up toℓ=8, for the 319
eccentricSXSwaveforms used in this work, shown as a function
of the mass ratioqand earliest GW eccentricitye gw measured by
