modes also requires improvement in future developments.
When transitioning from the (2,2)-mode metric to the SNR-
weighted metric, the median of maximum mismatches in-
creases from 0.030% to 0.081% (a factor of∼2.7) for
SEOBNRv5EHM, from 0.023% to 0.084% (a factor of∼3.6)
forSEOBNRv6EHM, and from 0.37% to 1.48% (a factor of∼4)
forTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı. Since the degradation of mismatches
when including higher-order modes is larger inSEOBNRv6EHM
than inSEOBNRv5EHM, this suggests that the prescription for
higher-order modes inSEOBNRv6EHMmay not be the most ad-
equate. As discussed in Secs. V A and VII B 1, there is a sig-
nificant freedom in how the higher-order modes are modeled;
future work could focus on studying more accurate prescrip-
tions. However, we emphasize that the improvement for large
eccentricities is also observed when higher-order modes are
included. Thus,SEOBNRv6EHMprovides an overall better ap-
proximation for the full GW signal of eccentric binaries.
The contribution of higher-order modes to the full wave-
form is, however, independent of eccentricity. Figure 14,
shows the sky-and-polarization-averaged SNR-weighted mis-
match between NR waveforms including only the (2,2) mode
and those including all modes up toℓ=8, for an inclina-
tionι s =π/3, as a function of the mass ratio and initial
GW eccentricity (only NR waveforms are used to disentan-
gle the results from inaccuracies in waveform models). It is
well known that large mass ratios and non-negligible incli-
nations enhance the contributions of higher-order modes in
QC binaries (e.g., [216]). This is also the case for eccentric
binaries, as shown in Fig. 14, where we observe a baseline
mismatch of∼1%, increasing with mass ratio up to∼20%.
However, the mismatch shows no dependence on eccentric-

32
ity.18 Indeed, orbital eccentricity does not introduce asym-
metries on the sphere (ι, φ) in whichh + andh × are expanded
in spherical-harmonic modesh ℓm. Rather, eccentric binaries
introduce an additional timescale characterized by the radial
oscillations.19 Therefore, the increase in mismatch with ec-
centricity observed in the bottom panels of Fig. 11 is entirely
driven by inaccuracies in the binary phasing, rather than by
the impact of higher-order modes or the absence of additional
multipoles. These results further suggest that improvements
in the modeling of higher-order modes for QC binaries will
be beneficial regardless of the eccentricity.
To complement this discussion about higher-order modes,
we provide in the bottom panel of Fig. 7 histograms of maxi-
mumSEOBNRv6EHMSNR-weighted mismatches for all the ec-
centric NR waveforms with (dark purple bins) and without
(green bins) them=0 multipoles, forℓ≤4. We also display
the estimated NR errors computed as the SNR-weighted mis-
matches between the highest and second-highest resolutions
(always including them=0 modes), when available. Similar
to the case of QC binaries discussed in Sec. VII B 1, the in-
clusion of NRm=0 modes degrades the comparisons against
NR, but the highest-mismatch cases are dominated by other
sources of error (e.g., large spins, large eccentricities), rather
than the absence ofm=0 modes inSEOBNRv6EHM. Although
other sources of error (e.g., inaccuracies in the RR force)
currently play a more significant role in limiting the accu-
racy for moderate-to-high eccentricities, low-eccentricity sys-
tems would immediately benefit from the modeling ofm=0
modes. We leave this for future work.
We conclude by summarizing our results as follows:
•SEOBNRv6EHMmatches the accuracy ofSEOBNRv5EHM
at low eccentricities, while at high eccentricities it can
be up to an order of magnitude more accurate.
•SEOBNRv6EHMis overall an order of magnitude more
accurate thanTEOBResumS-Dal´ ıfor all eccentricities.
•The improvements ofSEOBNRv6EHMat high eccentric-
ities come solely from new analytical results, and not
from calibrations to eccentric NR simulations.20
18 See also Fig. 11 of Ref. [201] for analogous results restricted to certain
modes up toℓ=5 and mass ratios up toq=4, obtained with a wave-
form approximant constructed from a NR surrogate model. Addition-
ally, the impact of neglecting higher-order modes in eccentric parameter-
estimation analyses has recently been explored in Ref. [380], where high
mass ratios and inclinations lead to larger biases. The role of eccentricity,
however, is less clear and requires further investigation.
19 The higher-order modes of waveform decompositions based onradial
frequenciesdo become more important as eccentricity increases. This is
the case for the typical decompositions employed in extreme-mass-ratio
eccentric waveform models (e.g., see Fig. 4 in Ref. [207]).
20 While no explicit numerical calibration is performed, it is important to
note that different analytical choices were made based on comparisons
against eccentric NR waveforms.
3. Generic planar binaries
Modeling the GW emission from dynamically captured bi-
naries or from scattering encounters poses a great challenge,
since they probe higher velocities than QC or moderately-
eccentric systems, as observed in the dynamics from NR sim-
ulations shown Fig. 5. The relative velocity of the consid-
ered dynamically captured binary (violet) reaches∼55% the
speed of light during the first and second periastron passages,
at which the binary’s separation is∼5M. Similarly, the rel-
ative velocity of the shown scattered binary (orange) reaches
∼65% the speed of light during the encounter at a separation
of∼5M. By contrast, the QC and eccentric (bound) systems
reach∼60% the speed of lightonlyat the end of the plunge.
To quantify the accuracy ofSEOBNRv6EHMfor generic
planar-orbit configurations, we compare with NR simula-
tions. Specifically, we compare with the NR waveforms
from a dynamical capture (SXS:BBH:4000), a 90 ◦ scatter-
ing encounter (SXS:BBH:3999), and a 180 ◦ scattering en-
counter (SXS:BBH:4292), all publicly-available, equal-mass,
nonspinning simulations from theSXScatalog [286]. Addi-
tionally, we employ data (predicted scattering angles) from
Ref. [381], comprising 61SXSNR scattering encounters span-
ning a wide range of binary configurations, with varying
energy, angular momentum, spins, and mass ratios. In all
the comparisons, we also include the predictions from the
TEOBResumS-Dal´ ımodel [259].
The waveforms comparisons are provided in Fig. 15. The
first set of panels (left column of the page) is dedicated to the
dynamical capture, while the second and third sets of panels
(right column of the page) focus on the∼90 ◦ (top) and∼180 ◦
(bottom) scattering encounters. In each set of panels, we dis-
play the real part and amplitude of the non-vanishing modes,
for the corresponding NR simulation (gray), along with the
SEOBNRv6EHM(red) andTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı(yellow) predic-
tions. To generate the waveforms, both models are initialized
with the ADM energy and total angular momentum from the
NR metadata, at an initial separation of 103M.
The results in Fig. 15 suggest a better qualitative perfor-
mance ofSEOBNRv6EHMwith respect toTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı.
For the dynamical capture case, we find thatSEOBNRv6EHM
yields better predictions for the time between periastron pas-
sages and the merger. This results in an improved over-
all agreement with the NR waveforms, including the higher-
order modes. We note, however, that the NR higher-order
modes exhibit unphysical features in the amplitude at early
times, associated with the waveform extrapolation proce-
dure used in the simulation. In contrast, the higher-order
modes ofSEOBNRv6EHMshow a smooth decay at early times
and remain qualitatively consistent with the NR results close
to merger, even reproducing the mode-mixing during the
merger-ringdown phase of the (3,2) mode. This is not the
case forTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı, where we see that the (4,4)
mode displays nonsmooth behavior aroundt=−1500M, as
well as amplitude zero crossings near merger. For scattering
waveforms,TEOBResumS-Dal´ ıalso develops unphysical fea-
tures in the higher-order modes aroundt≈ ±40M, whereas
SEOBNRv6EHMreproduces more closely the NR waveforms.

35
tering angles and predicted captures (dotted vertical lines)
fromSEOBNRv6EHMare closer to the NR data. However,
this improvement is less pronounced than in QC and eccen-
tric (bound) configurations. Remarkably, the best perfor-
mance is achieved bySEOBNRv5HM, as already reported in
Ref. [381].SEOBNRv5HMshares the same Hamiltonian struc-
ture asSEOBNRv6EHM, but has different calibration parameters
and employs a QC RR force. One might naively expect that
employing an eccentric RR force would improve the modeling
of scattering encounters, but this is not the case (at least not
with our chosen resummation). During the development of
SEOBNRv6EHM, we observed an improvement in the predicted
scattering angles when higher PN orders were included in the
eccentricity corrections to the RR force (beyond the 1PN order
used inSEOBNRv6EHM). However, as discussed in Sec. III B 4,
increasing the PN order of these corrections results in a degra-
dation of accuracy for bound systems.
Taken together, our findings for the generic planar-orbit
waveforms and scattering angles underscore the need for fur-
ther investigation into alternative resummations of the wave-
form modes and the RR force that accurately apply to both
bound and unbound binaries. This, together with a more sys-
tematic investigation of model accuracy for unbound orbits,
is left for future work. For now, conclusions drawn from
generic planar-orbit results obtained withSEOBNRv6EHMand
TEOBResumS-Dal´ ıshould be interpreted with caution.
We conclude thatSEOBNRv6EHMdemonstrates a strong per-
formance across all configurations, marking an important step
toward accurate models for generic-orbit binaries.
C. Computational performance
Waveform generation in eccentric models is computation-
ally more expensive than their QC counterparts due to the
extra equations and functions required to model eccentricity.
Furthermore, eccentric binaries depend on two extra param-
eters with respect to QC binaries (e.g., eccentricity and a ra-
dial phase). Thus, strategies and codes that reduce the overall
computational cost of eccentric models need to be developed.
SEOBNRv6EHMis implemented within the high-performance
and flexiblepySEOBNRPython package [224], which has been
optimized to reduce waveform generation time. The optimiza-
tions consisted mainly of reducing redundancy in the original
code (as used inSEOBNRv5models) and usingCython[382]
to speed up the evaluation of cost-intensive expressions. Fur-
thermore,SEOBNRv6EHMevolves only four equations of mo-
tion (15) and employs compact expressions for the RR force
and modes, compared toSEOBNRv5EHM, which evolves six
equations of motion and uses longer expressions.
Here, we quantify the speed ofSEOBNRv6EHMand com-
pare it with other state-of-the-art models across various bi-
nary configurations typically expected for current LVK detec-
tors. For all the models below, we include all their supported
modes up toℓ=4, with the remaining settings kept as default.
The benchmarks were performed with theHypatiacomputer
cluster at the Max Planck Institute for Gravitational Physics
in Potsdam, on a compute node equipped with a dual-socket

Figure 18. Histograms of waveform-evaluation walltimes for differ-
ent models and for randomly distributed QC (top panel) and eccentric
(bottom panel) binary configurations, initialized at an orbit-averaged
frequency of⟨f start⟩=10 Hz with a fixed sampling rate of 2048 Hz.
For the QC parameter space, we sample 5000 points withq∈[1,10],
M∈[5,100] M ⊙, and|χ i| ≤0.9 (i∈ {1,2}, aligned-spins). For the ec-
centric parameter space, we sample 10000 points with the same mass
and spin ranges, and withe∈[0,0.5] andζ∈[0,2π]. The vertical
dashed lines indicate the median of the corresponding histogram.
as discontinuities in the curves shown in Fig. 17, since smaller
sampling rates lead to faster waveform generations. For sys-
tems withe 0 >0, the discontinuities are less pronounced,
as the computational cost of modeling eccentricity dominates
over the differences caused by the change in sampling rate.
Related to this, for bothSEOBNRv5EHMandSEOBNRv6EHM, we
observe in Fig. 17 an increase of walltimes as the eccentricity
is augmented, until a certain point at which a further increase
of eccentricity decreases the generation time. This is counter-
intuitive to the fact that, at fixed orbit-averaged frequency, an
increase in eccentricity leads to a decrease in waveform dura-
tion due to larger losses of energy and angular momentum at
periastron passages. This effect was documented in Ref. [279]
and, in summary, can be explained by the balance between the
numerical integrator resolving the eccentricity timescales and
the waveforms becoming shorter as the eccentricity increases.
Additionally, we show in Fig. 18 histograms of the wall-
times for waveform generation (time-domain GW polariza-
tions) for randomly distributed configurations in the QC (top
panel) and eccentric (bottom panel) parameter spaces, for dif-
ferent waveform approximants. For the QC configurations, we
take 5000 random points withq∈[1,10],M∈[5,100] M ⊙,
|χ i| ≤0.9 (i∈ {1,2}, aligned-spins), while for the eccen-
tric configurations, we take 10000 random points with the
same bounds for masses and spins but withe∈[0,0.5]
andζ∈[0,2π]. For both cases, we start the waveforms at
⟨f start⟩=10 Hz with a fixed sampling rate of 2048 Hz.
In the zero-eccentricity limit, both figures show that
the performance ofSEOBNRv6EHMis markedly close to
that ofSEOBNRv5HM, and is significantly better than that
ofSEOBNRv5PHMandSEOBNRv5EHM. These are remarkable
properties, as theSEOBNRv6EHMcode includes several addi-
tional functions for modeling generic planar-orbit waveforms.
Particularly,SEOBNRv6EHMevolvestwobinary dynamics (a
main dynamics and a background QC evolution that helps
in the construction of inspiral-merger-ringdown waveforms;
see Sec. V B). In fact,SEOBNRv5HMis faster only because it
uses a post-adiabatic (PA) evolution of the dynamics. In con-
trast,SEOBNRv6EHMdoes not use any simplification for the
main binary dynamics (it only uses a PA prescription for the
background QC evolution). This encourages the development
of analytical methods to accelerate the EOB dynamics of ec-
centric binaries [383], since they will bring QC and eccentric
models to a common computational performance.
For eccentric configurations, the computational efficiency
ofSEOBNRv6EHMoutperforms that ofSEOBNRv5EHMand
TEOBResumS-Dal´ ı. Figure 17 shows explicitly that,
forallcases,SEOBNRv6EHMis significantly faster than
SEOBNRv5EHM. Both models have a similar definition of ec-
centricity (differing only in the RR gauge), yielding nearly
equal-duration waveforms for the same input parameters (with
the exception of high-eccentricity systems, not included in
these tests). This justifies a fair timing comparison between
the two models when evaluated at the same point in param-
eter space. In contrast, due to a different eccentricity treat-
ment, waveforms generated withTEOBResumS-Dal´ ıare typi-
cally shorter than those ofSEOBNRv6EHM(andSEOBNRv5EHM)
when given the same input parameters. This precludes a com-
parison similar to that in Fig. 17, unless the eccentric param-
eters of one of the models are optimized to find the most ad-
equate waveform for each considered case. For this reason,
we find it more convenient to compare models by evaluat-
ing them at random points across a given region of parameter
space, as shown in Fig. 18. There, it is straightforward to see
the waveform-evaluation improvement ofSEOBNRv6EHMover
SEOBNRv5EHM(by factors of∼2−4) andTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı
(by factors of∼2−6) across all configurations, including bina-
ries with high mass ratios and small total masses, which com-

37
pose the tail of high walltimes in the histograms of Fig. 18.21
These results indicate thatSEOBNRv6EHMis faster than all
EOB eccentric models. Our benchmarks are complemented
by results from the companion manuscript [287]. There, it
is shown thatSEOBNRv6EHMcan be up to∼3 times faster
thanSEOBNRv5EHMin GW parameter estimation. Hence, we
expectSEOBNRv6EHMto open the possibility of cost-intensive
investigations that require accurate eccentric waveforms.
D. Robustness across parameter space
Studying the behavior of a waveform model across the bi-
nary parameter space is crucial for establishing its reliability.
Here, we analyze the robustness ofSEOBNRv6EHMby identify-
ing the parameter space regions where we expect the model to
generate physically meaningful waveforms, and by studying
waveform changes under variations of the input parameters.
Determining initial conditions for generic planar orbits is
challenging, as one must know precise instantaneous values of
the relative separation and momenta of the binary. To ensure
physically accurate initial conditions,SEOBNRv6EHMimposes
a range of constraints, as discussed in Sec. IV. The excluded
initial conditions are always associated with challenging con-
figurations, for example, very small separations or states very
close to merger. As an example, we show in Fig. 19 the pre-
dicted initial separation for an equal-mass, nonspinning bi-
nary as a function of the starting eccentricity and frequency,
as predicted bySEOBNRv6EHM, complemented with a PN for-
mula (see below). The top (middle) panel shows the predicted
separation when the binary is initialized at periastron (ζ=0)
[apastron (ζ=π)]. For reference, besides the dimensionless
orbit-averaged orbital frequency⟨MΩ⟩, we also indicate the
total massMof the binary when the physical orbit-averaged
GW frequency is equal to 10 Hz. The red dashed curves delin-
eate the regions whereSEOBNRv6EHMwaveform generation is
successful (below the curves) from those where it fails (above
the curves); these curves were calculated numerically, by it-
erating model evaluations over the eccentricity. The sepa-
ration is calculated using Eq. (34c) of Ref. [278], 22 but we
verified that plotting the trueSEOBNRv6EHMpredicted sepa-
ration results in the same plots (for the regions below the red
dashed curves). Additionally, in the bottom panels, we include
the EOB trajectory and the (2,2) mode for one representative
boundary case (indicated by a pink cross in the middle panel).
For other configurations (unequal mass ratios, nonzero spins,

or different frequencies), the qualitative behavior of the initial
separation is analogous to the one observed in Fig. 19.
It is crucial to note that not all input values (⟨MΩ⟩,e, ζ)
will produce a successful waveform generation. Some com-
binations are not physically meaningful (e.g., initial separa-
tions below 2M), or correspond to configurations resembling
head-on collisions. This is illustrated in the bottom panels of
Fig. 19, which show that higher eccentricities result in shorter-
duration systems that fall outside the model’s assumptions.
QC models overcome this issue by exploiting the system’s
circular symmetry, thereby determining initial conditions at
lower frequencies (which still correspond to thesamephysi-
cal system). An eccentric binary, however, cannot be trivially
evolved backward as its dynamical state depends not only on
the frequency, but on the eccentricity and radial anomaly.
This is a problem common to all eccentric models, and it
is important to take it into account when drawing conclusions
from their results (e.g., when observing posterior railing in
parameter estimation [103]). TheSEOBNRv5EHMmodel ad-
dresses this problem by integrating backward a set of PN sec-
ular evolution equations whenever the starting separation is
smaller than 10M. In this way, EOB initial conditions can be
determined at an earlier (less challenging) stage of the evo-
lution. This approach was needed inSEOBNRv5EHMto avoid
issues with its equations of motion. Nonetheless, a study as-
sessing its impact (e.g., in parameter estimation) has not yet
been performed. Such method, however, turned out to be
highly non-user-friendly, as it gives the model an unexpected
behavior from the user perspective, and its purpose is not ob-
vious to someone outside the eccentric waveform modeling
field. For these reasons, and thanks to the simpler analytical
structure of theSEOBNRv6EHMequations of motion, we do not
adopt the secular backward integration approach (not to be
confused with the backward evolution of thefullequations of
motion, as explained in footnote 6). This results in a smoother
model behavior across the parameter space. As demonstrated
in the companion manuscript [287], this choice does not neg-
atively affect parameter estimation, as all the runs there were
executed seamlessly (some even with fewer waveform genera-
tion failures thanSEOBNRv5EHM), including high-eccentricity
injection-recovery analyses with upper priors on eccentricity
of 0.9 (we also used an upper prior on eccentricity of 0.99
for some additional analyses, finding no issues with the runs
or their convergence). Thus,SEOBNRv6EHMenables accurate
high-eccentricity parameter estimation studies.
Apart from the excluded challenging initial configurations,
the parameter-space coverage ofSEOBNRv6EHMhasnoinher-
ent restrictions. TheSEOBNRv5EHMmodel had to be con-
strained to a conservative region of parameter space to avoid
a “desynchronization” of its equations of motion that can po-
tentially appear for highly-eccentric, long-lived systems (see
Appendix E of Ref. [279]). InSEOBNRv6EHM, no such con-
striction is needed, as the equations of motion (15) are well-
defined and self-consistent (this is one of the major improve-
ments with respect toSEOBNRv5EHM). Thus,SEOBNRv6EHM
can be used anywhere in parameter space, with the usual
caveats of models. For example, we expect reduced accuracy
for configurations that differ significantly from those used to
calibrate the model (e.g., high mass ratios or large spins; see
Sec. VI), and reduced accuracy as eccentricity increases.
Building on the LVK review tests carried out for
SEOBNRv5EHM, we successfully assessed the robustness of
SEOBNRv6EHMacross broad parameter space regions covering
mass ratiosq∈[1,100], the full spin range, and eccentric pa-
rameters (e, ζ) for bound orbits, as well as (r,E/M,J/M 2) for
generic planar orbits. Namely, we performed visual inspection
of the waveforms, stability under perturbations of the input
parameters, checks of the mode hierarchy (ensuring that the
(2,2) mode has the largest amplitude), and verification of the
monotonicity of the inspiral amplitude and frequency evolu-
tion for the QC (2,2) mode, as well as of the envelope defined
by the maxima and minima of the eccentric (2,2) mode.
For example, in Fig. 20, we show the smooth changes
of the amplitude of all theSEOBNRv6EHMwaveform modes,
(ℓ,|m|)={(2,2),(3,3),(2,1),(4,4),(3,2),(4,3)}, under uni-
form variations of the eccentric parameterse∈[0,0.7] (left
panels),ζ∈[0,2π] (relativistic anomaly, middle panels), and
l∈[0,2π] (mean anomaly, right panels), at a dimensionless
orbit-averaged orbital frequency of⟨Mω⟩ ≈0.01547 (equiv-
alent, e.g., to an initial GW frequency⟨f start⟩=20 Hz for a
total massM=50 M ⊙) for a system with mass ratioq=3,
and dimensionless spin componentsχ 1 =0.6 andχ 2 =−0.3.
In the left panels of Fig. 20, we observe the expected de-
crease in the time to merger with increasing eccentricity at
fixed starting frequency, while in the middle and right panels,
we observe the amplitude oscillations induced by the radial
anomaly. The middle panels show that a uniform distribution
on relativistic anomaly leads to a clustering of the amplitude
around periastron atζ∈ {0,2π}. In contrast, the right pan-
els show that a uniform distribution on mean anomaly leads
to evenly distributed waveform amplitudes. These observa-
tions are complemented by Fig. 3 in Sec. IV A, where one
can see the effects of uniformly sampling in either of the two
anomalies (see also Fig. 5 of Ref. [142] for the effect of mean
anomaly in NR waveforms). In the context of surrogate mod-
eling of eccentric waveforms, the mean anomaly approach has
been quite fruitful [146, 384]. However, the impact of using
either anomaly on parameter estimation still needs to be ad-
dressed. For the present, we chose the relativistic anomalyζ
as the default radial phase parameter inSEOBNRv6EHM.
In Fig. 20, all cases show analogous behavior during
merger-ringdown due to the employed QC prescription. The
true behavior at merger has a dependence on eccentricity and
radial anomaly, as illustrated in Fig. 5 of Ref. [142] (see also
Refs. [140, 141, 143, 340, 385–387]). These effects are rela-
tively small, so we neglect them and leave their inclusion for
future work. Further investigation is required to characterize
the impact of eccentric merger-ringdown effects on waveform
accuracy and parameter estimation.

of the waveform polarizations,h + andh ×, under variations of
the input parametersE/MandJ/M 2 for generic planar orbits,
for systems with mass ratioq=3, dimensionless spin com-
ponentsχ 1 =0.3 andχ 2 =−0.4, initial separationr=500M,
and a line-of-sight inclination ofι=π/3. Specifically, the
top panel shows the changes with respect to a set of energy

A particularly interesting behavior observed in the top panel
of Fig. 21 is the transition from dynamical capture to a scatter-
ing encounter. Starting from the dynamically captured system
withE/M=1.0005 (dark blue curves), we observe that in-
creasing the energy delays the subsequent periastron passages
to later times, until the system becomes unbound (light green
curves, withE/M=1.0016). The precise threshold value be-
tween scattering and dynamical capture for this system lies
betweenE/M=1.00105 andE/M=1.0016. We remark
that the smoothness properties exemplified in Fig. 21 are im-
portant for studies analyzing dynamical-capture and scattering
waveforms [112, 113, 388–393]. As a proof of principle, in
the companion manuscript [287], we have successfully per-
formed parameter estimation over different high-mass events
using generic planar-orbit initial conditions.
Overall, the behavior ofSEOBNRv6EHMremains robust

41
across different parameter space regions. This robustness en-
sures the model can be used straightforwardly in different GW
studies. Better prescriptions for initial conditions are needed
to expand the parameter space of eccentric waveform models.
However, new data analysis techniques are also needed to en-
able robust eccentric GW inference. We expectSEOBNRv6EHM
to play a role in supporting these future investigations.
VIII. CONCLUSIONS
Eccentric GW models will play a key role in discriminating
compact binary formation channels and in achieving unbiased
parameter estimation. Here, we presentedSEOBNRv6EHM: a
new multipolar, time-domain, EOB model for the complete
GW radiation of comparable-mass BBHs orbiting in generic
planar orbits. The model leverages new resummations of the
RR force and waveform modes, and it provides the multi-
poles (ℓ,|m|)∈ {(2,2),(3,3),(2,1),(4,4),(3,2),(4,3)}for
the full inspiral-merger-ringdown evolution of coalescing bi-
naries, dynamical captures, and scattering encounters. The
model has been calibrated to QCSXSNR simulations, and it
constructs the merger-ringdown under a QC approximation.
SEOBNRv6EHMisaccurate. In the low-eccentricity limit,
its performance is comparable to that ofSEOBNRv5EHM[279],
while for the largest eccentricities it improves the accuracy
by up to an order of magnitude.SEOBNRv6EHMachieves a
median of∼0.02% for the maximum mismatches across the
total mass interval [20,200] M ⊙ when comparing against 319
eccentricSXSNR waveforms [134, 142, 286]. Importantly,
mismatches remain below∼2% even for the most eccentric
NR configurations. Relative toTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı[259], we
observe an overall improvement of about one order of mag-
nitude across all eccentricities. In the generic planar-orbit
regime, we compare against NR waveforms from one dynam-
ical capture and two scattering encounters, as well as against
61 scattering-angle results from Ref. [381], finding improved
agreement relative toTEOBResumS-Dal´ ı. The high accuracy
ofSEOBNRv6EHMis further demonstrated in the companion
manuscript [287], where its capability to accurately infer the
parameters of highly eccentric binaries is established through
injection-recovery studies with synthetic NR signals.
SEOBNRv6EHMiscomputationally efficient. We per-
formed benchmarks to assess the computational speed of
SEOBNRv6EHMacross a broad range of binary configura-
tions. Thanks to code optimizations and to a simpler an-
alytical structure,SEOBNRv6EHMcan be 2−6 times faster
thanSEOBNRv5EHMandTEOBResumS-Dal´ ıin single wave-
form evaluations, depending on the specific binary parame-
ters. The improved efficiency ofSEOBNRv6EHMin Bayesian
parameter estimation is demonstrated in the companion
manuscript [287], where the model is shown to be approxi-
mately three times faster than its predecessor,SEOBNRv5EHM.
SEOBNRv6EHMisrobust. The parameter space covered by
SEOBNRv6EHMhas no inherent restrictions, apart from systems
with very high eccentricity near merger. This is a large im-
provement overSEOBNRv5EHM, whose parameter space cov-
erage had to be restricted to avoid unphysical features in the
waveforms, mainly occurring for long-lived, highly eccentric
systems. Leveraging this robustness and the improved compu-
tational efficiency, the companion manuscript [287] presents
analyses of 26 GW events spanning a wide range of configu-
rations, including high mass ratios, low total masses (includ-
ing NSs), and large eccentricities. These results show that
SEOBNRv6EHMis readily applicable to standard GW analyses.
Several subsequent studies can be made to improve eccen-
tric EOB modeling. Further research on the RR force will be
essential, as it is one of the main accuracy bottlenecks. The
current paradigm of enforcing a QC factorization in resum-
mations of the RR force and waveform modes may, in fact, be
introducing limitations in the modeling of eccentric systems.
The sigmoid resummation introduced in this work may prove
useful for developing new approaches. Studying different
RR gauges could be important for achieving better accuracy
across different binary configurations. The final portion of the
waveform could be improved by incorporating recent develop-
ments in the modeling of the eccentric merger-ringdown phase
(e.g., [140–142, 265, 285, 340]). Developing a framework for
calibrating EOB models to eccentric NR simulations is a chal-
lenging but promising avenue that will require careful control
over the eccentric parameters. Finally, testing the impact of
post-Minkowskian results on the accuracy of inspiral-merger-
ringdown waveforms remains an open problem that has been
addressed only in the QC-orbit limit [227, 394].
Our next goal is to develop an accurate and efficient wave-
form model for generic-orbit,precessing-spinBBHs, using
SEOBNRv6EHMas baseline model [395]. In this context, there
has been recent work in developing inspiral-only PN wave-
form models [183–187], as well as inspiral-merger-ringdown
EOB waveform models [267, 275]. Such generic models will
be essential to allow forunbiasedBBH parameter estimation.

3. Eccentricity corrections to the RR force and the sigmoid resummation
PN expressions for the RR force tend to grow significantly during plunges or strong periastron passages, spoiling the numerical
integration of the equations of motion. Here, we introduce ansigmoid resummationwhich limits their growth.
We consider a PN expanded functionf PN which has been factorized as
f F ≡f fact f corr,(A16a)
f corr ≡1+
nX
i=1
ϵi∆f i,(A16b)
wheref fact is another factorization containing the leading-order (Newtonian) term off PN (and, potentially, additional higher PN
terms), and the functions∆f i are defined in such a way that the PN expansion off F coincides withf PN at a givenn-th PN order.
Then, the sigmoid resummation off corr is defined as
f exp ≡ 2
1+exp(f arg exp) ,(A17a)
f arg exp ≡
nX
i=1
ϵi∆f arg exp
i ,(A17b)
where the corrections∆f arg exp
i make the PN expansion off exp to coincide withf corr. At 3PN order, a straightforward calculation
(a Taylor expansion in the PN parameterϵ) yields
∆f arg exp

Thus, given the PN coefficients∆fi, these formulas give the elements entering into the argument of the exponential. Importantly,
if corrections tof PN start at 1PN order (n=2), then∆f 1 =0, and Eqs. (A18) simplify significantly.
In this way, we can resum the PN-expanded functionf PN as
f F, exp ≡f fact 2
1+exp(f arg exp) ,(A19)
from which we can observe the following properties:
•f F, exp →f fact whenf arg exp →0, e.g., in the Newtonian, low-velocity limit.
•f F, exp →0 orf F, exp →2f fact whenf arg exp → ±∞, respectively, e.g., during strong periastron passages or the plunge.
The connection with the RR force (42) is immediate: after extracting a factor which contains the leading order RR force  f fact ∈ F modes

We note that the eccentricity corrections withℓ+modd have the leading-order contributionh ecc,LO
ℓm =r 2/3/p4/3
ϕ,N, instead of
hecc,LO
ℓm =1 as theℓ+meven modes. This discrepancy comes from the definition of ˆS effin Eq. (63). With such definition, one
has ˆS eff∼1+O(1/c 2) in theℓ+modd caseonlyfor QC orbits, for whichp ϕ →1/v+O(1/c 2). This is not surprising considering
that the factor ˆS effwas introduced for QC binaries in Refs. [288, 311, 312]. If one keeps the idea of having corrections in the
mode factorization [Eqs. (61) and (62)] that become unity at Newtonian order, then one could consider the definition
ˆS eff=
( Heff, ℓ+meven,
pϕ/(r2Ω), ℓ+modd, (A23)

50
which satisfies ˆS eff∼1+O(1/c 2) for generic planar orbits. We do not implement this new definition inSEOBNRv6EHMsince
a change in ˆS effwould propagate to thef qc
ℓm factors. We leave for future work the derivation of expressions consistent with the
definition in Eq. (A23) and the study of their impact on the accuracy of the modes.
5. Orbital frequency for circular orbits
The 5PN expression for the orbital frequency of circular orbitsΩ c expressed as function of the separationr, discussed in
Sec. III B 1 and used in the RR force (42) and waveform modes (61), is calculated by perturbatively solving the equation
˙pr =0=∂H EOB/∂p ϕ |pr=0 forp ϕ =p ϕ(r), and then replacing the solution intoΩ c ≡∂H EOB/∂p ϕ |pr=0, followed by a further
expansion. This results in the following PN expression:
Ωc ≡Ω c,S 0 + Ωc,S 1 + Ωc,S 2 + Ωc,S 3 + Ωc,S 4 ,(A24a)
